## I. INTRODUCTION

In a well-spirited effort to utilize quantum devices to the best of their abilities, the common approach could be summarized as: Do as little as possible on the quantum computer by offloading work onto classical machines. However, this does not relieve quantum computers from the necessary and sufficient conditions to run quantum circuits that are both i) classically hard and ii) practically useful. With classical algorithms still outperforming current quantum computers for dynamical simulation [1-7], recent developments in variational quantum computing [8] and no clear trajectory for making use of near-term quantum devices, we are left to wonder: should we offload more work to classical computers?

In this work, we provide theoretical guarantees and practical implementations of fully classical and quantumenhanced classical simulations of quantum landscape patches. That is, for simulating a small region in the full expectation landscape spanned by the gate angles of a quantum circuit. We consider a spectrum of classicality, where quantum computers are used either i) not at all, ii) to provide measurements of the initial states, or iii) to estimate the most important contributions to a quantum landscape. In the former two cases, our results can be viewed as providing guarantees for the simulation of lowmagic or near-Clifford circuits [9]. In the latter two cases, the quantum computer is effectively tasked with collecting data to build a so-called classical surrogate [2, 10-14] of the patch of a quantum landscape. As such, our work establishes a hybrid quantum-classical simulation framework that attempts to realize the original motivation of utilizing quantum resources only for the smallest possible but crucial component.

∗ The first three authors contributed equally to this work.

Our results are applicable to variational quantum computing where the landscape corresponds to a loss function which one seeks to minimize to solve a given task [15, 16]. In this context, prior studies of classical surrogates include those for; shallow hardware efficient ansätze [1720], circuits with polynomial dynamical Lie Algebra [2125], unstructured parameterized quantum circuits [2, 26], quantum convolutional neural networks [26, 27], quantum kernel-based circuits [28], noisy circuits [14, 29] and quantum machine learning [10-12, 30]. However, those proposals are to surrogate the entire landscape for relatively restricted families of circuits. Surrogating more general circuits in restricted regions, except for some largely heuristic works [31, 32], has so far not been studied. In this paper, we show that it is possible to create a classical surrogate for previously proposed reduced angle range initialization strategies for avoiding barren plateaus [33-42].

We emphasize that although these surrogates superficially have the flavor of dequantizations, they do not in general imply the irrelevance of quantum computers. In the generic setting, one still requires a quantum device to generate the data from which the surrogate is constructed, except in those cases where the relevant data can themselves be obtained by efficient classical simulation. In this sense, the patch-surrogate approach enables a training paradigm of a similar spirit to Ref. [31], in which quantum resources are devoted solely to the construction and updating of the surrogates. We investigate this setting and compare its performance with that of the standard gradient-descent approach. Although our preliminary results do not yield definitive conclusions as there exist regimes in which each method outperforms the other, this observation points to a promising new direction for exploration. In particular, surrogates may offer significant practical benefits given the effect of noise drift and in the context queuing times for cloud-based hardware.

Beyond variational quantum computing, our results have implications in numerous domains including quantum sensing and quantum simulation. For quantum sensing, one could use our surrogated landscape for inferencebased techniques [43, 44]. In the case of dynamical simulation, the landscape is that of physical expectation values spanned by the system parameters and can be used to analyze hardware data or extend coherence times. Our numerical simulations, and in particular the long-time simulation of a 127-qubit transverse field Ising model on a heavy-hex topology, demonstrate our algorithms' utility beyond the regimes in which we have strict analytic guarantees.

## II. BACKGROUND

We consider the task of simulating expectation values of the form

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \text {Tr} [ \mathcal { E } _ { \alpha } ( \rho ) O ] \, , \quad \quad ( 1 ) \quad \begin{matrix} \text {reg} \\ \text {or} \end{matrix}
$$

where ρ is an n-qubit input state, O is a Hermitian operator, and Eα is a quantum channel parameterized by an m-component vector of real parameters α. By varying the choice in ρ, O and Eα this expression can be used to capture observables and losses for a wide range of variational quantum algorithms, real-time simulations, and quantum machine learning tasks [45, 46].

In the following, we will briefly provide some background on the notions of classical simulation and classical surrogatability. We will then introduce a classical algorithm for classical-quantum surrogation. In particular, these techniques constitute a new variant of LOWESA, introduced in Refs. [2, 14], and more generally belongs to the family of Pauli propagation algorithms [2, 4, 14, 26, 27, 47-55].

## A. Classical simulation and surrogation

By now, it is well established that a large class of parameterized quantum circuits exhibit the so-called barren plateau phenomenon where their parameter landscapes concentrate exponentially with system size [56, 57]. On a barren plateau, the probability that any randomly chosen parameter vector α corresponds to an observable expectation value which deviates significantly from its average vanishes exponentially with the number of qubits n upon which the circuit acts [58]. This is widely believed to prohibit the training with random initializations for a broad family of variational quantum algorithms [42, 57-72] and quantummachine learning models [73-82]. Nevertheless, it leaves open the possibility that small sub-regions of the landscape exhibit substantial landscape variances.

These realizations have prompted recent efforts into variance lower bounds for sub-regions of quantum landscapes. Early work investigated small angle initializations for the hardware efficient variational ansatz [33, 36, 37, 83, 84] and Hamiltonian variational ansatz [34, 85]. Subsequent work derived bounds for iterative learning schemes in the context of variational quantum simulation [38], as well as for ground state preparation [40]. These approaches have recently been unified in Ref. [41] and further extended to generative modeling with a nonlinear loss in Ref. [42]. Common to all these works is the observation that it is possible to guarantee landscape variances that vanish at worst polynomially in the number of trainable parameters in special sub-regions that have a radius that shrinks polynomially in the number of parameters.

In parallel to this line of work, it has been observed that there is a strong correlation between being able to prove that a variational quantum landscape is barren plateau free (i.e, proving that the variance of expectation values vanishes, at worst, polynomially) and the ability to create a classical surrogate for that landscape [8]. Here we demonstrate that in all the reduced parameter regimes where it is possible to provide polynomial (in n or m) lower bounds on the variance of the expectation value, it is possible to construct a classical surrogate of the expectation value landscape.

To pin down what we mean by a classical surrogate in this context, let us start by defining what it means to simulate an expectation value. We will say that an algorithm can classically simulate an expectation value f(α) with high probability if a polynomial time classical algorithm can implement a function f(α) approximating the expectation value up to error ϵ, i.e.,

$$
\Delta f ( \alpha ) \colon = \left | \overline { f } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | \leqslant \epsilon \, ,
$$

with probability 1-δ for random α ∼ P where P is some distribution of interest and where ϵ ∈ Θ(1) and δ ∈ Θ(1) are arbitrarily small constants. We will also be interested in computing the function for any parameter settings, i.e., the worst-case error. Thus, we will also consider a stronger version where an algorithm can compute the expectation function if Eq. (2) holds for all α in a given landscape region.

In contrast, preparing a classical surrogate involves using a quantum computer for an initial (polynomial-time) data acquisition phase. Since this data can be utilized

by subsequent classical simulation, the combination is more powerful than a purely classical algorithm. More concretely, we say it is possible to efficiently "classically surrogate" a quantum expectation function f(α) over the distribution α ∼ P if, using data from an initial polynomial-time data acquisition phase on a quantum computer, it is subsequently possible to classically simulate expectation values over P.

Crucially, the practical benefits of classically surrogating a quantum landscape depend on the family of channels that need to be run on the quantum computer in the non-adaptive polynomial-time data acquisition phase. For our most general results on the surrogatability of quantum landscapes, we will allow arbitrary polynomial depth and width circuits to be run. In this case, shown in Fig. 1a), the main advantage in constructing the classical surrogate landscape is that after an initial overhead, any subsequent algorithm can be run fully classically. Such a scheme transforms a potentially adaptive hybrid quantum-classical algorithm into a non-adaptive one, opening up the possibility of using the power of classical optimization tools such as automatic differentiation, while leaving the total quantum resources requirements unchanged. However, we will also consider more specialized cases where one only needs to perform Pauli measurements on the initial state. In this case, shown in Fig. 1b), the quantum resource requirements are substantially reduced as compared to running the original quantum channel to evaluate different points on the expectation landscape. This quantum resource reduction is achieved by offloading much of the work of simulating the circuit to a classical simulation algorithm.

It is possible to consider many different possible distributions P of parameters over sub-regions of a quantum landscape here. Here, in line with the small-angle initialization literature [34, 36, 38, 39], we will focus on uniform distributions over hypercubes of width 2r. More concretely, let us define

$$
\begin{array} { c c c } M o r e & & \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) \coloneqq \{ \alpha \} \text { such that } \alpha _ { i } \in [ \alpha _ { i } ^ { * } - r , \alpha _ { i } ^ { * } + r ] \ \forall \ i , \\ & & \\ \text {over} & & \\ & & 1 \ \text {, } & 1
$$

as the hypercube of parameter space centered around the point α∗ , andDm(α∗ , r) := Unif[Vm(α∗ , r)] (4)

as a uniform distribution over the hypercube Vm(α∗ , r). We expect that our conclusions straightforwardly generalize to other reduced parameter range distributions including Gaussian distributions [33, 37] or to allow for independent perturbation radii for each parameter.

## B. Pauli Propagation

There exist numerous classical techniques to simulate quantum circuits and evaluate the expectation function in Eq. (1). In this work, we employ Pauli propagation methods [2, 4, 14, 26, 27, 49, 50, 55, 86, 87]. In this context, it is most natural (and most efficient) to consider quantum circuits written as parameterized perturbations from Clifford circuits. That is, unitaries of the form

$$
U _ { \text {CP} } \left ( \alpha \right ) = \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } C _ { i } U _ { i } ( \alpha _ { i } ) \, ,
$$

where {Ci}mi=1 are a set of fixed but arbitrary Clifford unitaries and {Ui(αi) = e-iαiPi/2}mi=1 are parameterized rotations generated by Pauli operators {Pi}mi=1 . This architecture can be universal even when Ui are singlequbit phase rotations, though we allow Ui to be Pauli rotations acting on any number of qubits. In our analytics we will consider both the case where the parameters αi are uncorrelated and cases where they are correlated. We further emphasise however that this particular circuit form is inessential to our most general complexity bounds in Section III which can, for example, also quantify expectation landscapes for parameterized channels, and for circuit which are not near-Clifford.

Pauli propagation methods typically work in the Heisenberg picture where the initial operator (here the observable O) is often sparse in the Pauli basis. In this basis, the operator is back-propagated through the circuit and finally overlapped with the initial state ρ. In particular, since the adjoint action of any Clifford operation on any Pauli observable Pj simply produces another Pauli P ′ j ,

$$
C _ { i } ^ { \dagger } \, P _ { j } \, C _ { i } = s _ { i j } P _ { j } ^ { \prime } , \ \ s _ { i j } \in \{ - 1 , 1 \} , \quad ( 6 ) \quad F I G \\
$$

which we will notate as Pj Ci -→ ±P ′ j . These transformations can either be computed in O(n2 ) time using the Gottesman-Knill theorem [88] or in O(1) time for any a priori known Ci acting on few qubits by use of look-up tables. Applying a non-Clifford Pauli rotation e-iαiPi/2 to Pj leaves the operator invariant if [Pi, Pj] = 0, but otherwise produces a real-valued weighted sum of two Pauli operators,

$$
P _ { j } \xrightarrow { e ^ { \ i \alpha _ { i } P _ { i } / 2 } } \cos ( \alpha _ { i } ) P _ { j } + \sin ( \alpha _ { i } ) P _ { j } ^ { \prime \prime } \, , \quad ( 7 )
$$

where P ′′ j = i[Pi, Pj]/2 is a Pauli operator. We note that the sign of the sine term is flipped in the Schrödinger picture. By iterating this procedure, one ends up with a collection of Pauli operators and corresponding trigonometric prefactors.

After Pauli propagation, the expectation function in Eq. (1) takes the form

$$
f \left ( \alpha \right ) = \sum _ { P } c _ { P } \left ( \alpha \right ) \text {Tr} [ \rho P ] \, , \quad \quad \ \ ( 8 ) \quad
$$

where cP are the coefficients of the back-propagated Pauli operators P. These coefficients capture both the initial weight of each of the relevant Paulis in the target observable O and the sine and cosine coefficients that have been picked up during the propagation.

One approach, embracing the concept of expectation function surrogates [2, 14, 27], is not to compute the coefficients cP of the Pauli paths from scratch every time the parameters α are changed. Instead, one can symbolically or graphically represent the coefficients for each final Pauli P. When it becomes clear which P contribute significantly to the expectation in Eq. (8), only their corresponding coefficients cP can be re-evaluated. The approach has a substantial initial time and especially memory overhead, but once performed, enables rapid reevaluation of the expectation value f(α) for any α. The implementation used in this work can be seen as an improved variant of the original proposal of LOWESA. We give a clarifying illustration of the technique in Fig. 2.

In its raw form, the number of Pauli operators in the backpropagated observable (what we call the "Pauli paths"), or equivalently the number of terms in the sum in Eq. (8), grows exponentially with the number m of Pauli rotations in the circuit. To make the algorithm efficient for larger m values, one must therefore apply truncation schemes. Truncating higher weight Pauli operators has been shown to be efficient for both noisy circuits [14, 48, 52] and random circuits [26]. Here we will provide guarantees for a small-angle truncation scheme for patches of expectation landscapes corresponding to perturbations away from a Clifford circuit. We note that this approach is related to the techniques numerically demonstrated in Ref. [50].

## III. GENERAL PATCHES

## A. Error guarantees

Our first result is a general bound which shows that it is always possible to create a classical surrogate for small sub-regions of a quantum landscape generated by a general parametrized quantum channel. Concretely, we show that for any function that depends on m independent parameters, it is possible to create a surrogate of a patch of width ∼ 1/√m, provided that some assumptions on the magnitude of the higher order derivatives are satisfied. This result is captured by Theorem 1 below, which we prove and state more formally in Appendix C (Supplemental Theorem 3).

Theorem 1 (General Surrogation Guarantee, Informal). Consider an expectation function f(α) of the form in Eq. (1), where the measurement operator satisfies ∥O∥∞ ∈ O(1), and where the parameters of the quantum channel are taken from a hypercube region Vm(α∗ , r) around an arbitrary point α∗ of width 2r. Assume that the partial derivatives within this region scale at most exponentially in the derivative order k, i.e.,

$$
| \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ) | & \leqslant \gamma ^ { k } \| O \| _ { \infty } \quad , \ \forall \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) \ , \ \ ( 9 ) \\ f _ { \alpha } ( \alpha ) & = \alpha ( \sigma _ { 1 } ) \ \subset \mathcal { O } ( 1 ) \quad , \ \ \Gamma _ { \alpha } ( \sigma _ { 1 } ) = \mathcal { O } ( 1 )
$$

for some constant γ ∈ O(1). Furthermore, assume that the partial derivatives for k ∈ O(1) can be estimated efficiently on a quantum computer. Then, it is possible to efficiently classically surrogate f(α) up to an arbitrary small error ϵ ∈ Θ(1) with high probability for random α ∼Dm(α∗ , r) for

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { m } } \right ) , & & \quad ( 1 0 ) & & \quad \text {th} \\
$$

and for all α in Vm(α∗ , r) for

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { m } \right ) , \quad & \quad ( 1 1 ) \quad \text {the} \quad \\
$$

where in both cases there is an arbitrarily small but constant failure probability from shot noise in the data collection phase.

The proof of Theorem 1 relies on expressing the expectation value f(α) with Taylor's expansion around the point of interest α∗ and then showing that with some mild assumptions regarding the partial derivatives (see Assumptions 1-3 in Appendix C), a low-order truncation of the expansion gives a sufficiently good surrogate for a small region1 . These assumptions are in fact met by most parameterized quantum circuits considered currently by the community (see Appendix C1 f) and could also be applied to parameterized channels. In particular, it includes not just the uncorrelated Pauli rotation ansätze in Eq. (5) but also arbitrarily deep circuits where the m independent parameters can appear in multiple gates. To name just one example, the theorem also holds for gates using Givens Rotations, which are crucial elementary building blocks for quantum chemistry [89].

It is noteworthy that our average case surrogacy guarantee aligns with the growing body of prior work on absence of barren plateau proofs for patches of a quantum expectation function landscape that scale as 1/√m. In particular, it has been shown in Refs. [38, 39] that a region with r ∈ O(1/√m) around some minimum α∗ has substantial gradients

$$
V a r _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) } \left [ f ( \alpha ) \right ] \in \Omega \left ( \frac { 1 } { \text {poly} ( n ) } \right ) \, . \quad \text {(12)} \quad \text {of an} \quad \text {if} \quad \text {of any} \quad \text {is} \quad \text {q}
$$

1 We note that surrogation beyond r ∈ O(1/√m) is also achievable with low error, by extending the truncation order κ to be polynomial. This, in turn, increases the computational resources required to be super-polynomial or even exponential with m. Detailed derivations for various truncations are provided in Appendix C1g. Furthermore, these techniques could be adapted to other geometries. Although a rigorous generalization requires more technical work, Fig. 3 offers evidence that the results of Theorem 1 remain valid.

Theorem 1 hence implies that, in all of the sub-regions where it is possible to prove absence of barren plateaus, it is not necessary to run a variational quantum algorithm adaptively on a quantum computer but rather one could first quantumly prepare a surrogate which is then optimised fully classically.

Beyond the average case surrogatability guarantee, we additionally prove the worst-case error incurred by our algorithm in a smaller patch of width O(1/m). Inside this smaller patch, there exists no parameter vector α which is classically simulated with an error worse than the constant, but arbitrarily small, chosen error. As discussed further in the Appendices below Theorem 7, this implies that in the context of dynamical simulation Trotter errors can be reduced with only a polynomial overhead.

Theorem 1 leaves open the nature of the measurements required on the quantum computer, and in general these might require running highly non-trivial circuits. Namely, one will generally need to run circuits to evaluate the loss in order to compute gradients via the generalized parameter-shift rule [90]. Concretely, for general circuits where the patch is expanded around an arbitrary point α∗ , the quantum device can be used to estimate the function f(α) at different choices in α and the surrogate is generated by reconstructing a multi-variate Taylor expansion of f(α). In this case, the surrogate bears similarities to applying higher-order gradient optimization methods because generating the surrogate requires effectively measuring higher order derivatives of the loss. In this sense the distinction between running a variational quantum algorithm explicitly (by say updating by one step per iteration using a higher order gradient descent method) and generating the surrogate (by first measuring higher order gradients and then training classically for a few steps) can become blurred.

Nonetheless, Theorem 1 suggests it may be worthwhile to forego experimentally re-evaluating a quantum circuit with varying parameters at each step, and instead spend the quantum resources preparing a surrogate. The constructed surrogate can then be trained entirely classically for a few iterations within the patch. Nevertheless, for a whole training process to remain faithful, the surrogate will need to be reconstructed once the training trajectory leaves the patch.

In Appendix D, we present a preliminary exploration of this aspect. Specifically, we investigate the performance of an iterative surrogate training strategy and compare its quantum resource requirements-namely, the number of measurement shots-with those of standard gradient descent. We emphasize that, in this case, the surrogate is constructed directly at the level of the loss gradient, in contrast to the approaches discussed in the main text, where the loss function itself is surrogated. In addition, a shot-noise analysis of this gradient-based surrogate, including a practical approximation of the optimal shotallocation strategy for reducing shot-noise, is provided in Appendix D4. In the next subsection we will explore one key component of this approach - namely, we investigate numerically how far it is possible to train in practice using the data obtained to build a surrogate.

## B. Numerical tests

In this section we numerically investigate the maximum distance we can move in the parameter space before the surrogate becomes unusable. That is, the maximum distance such that training on the surrogate continues to decrease the true loss.

For concreteness, we consider a surrogate of an expectation loss function of the form of Eq. (1), where the observable is a Heisenberg Hamiltonian

$$
O = \frac { 1 } { 3 ( n - 1 ) } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n - 1 } ( X _ { i } X _ { i + 1 } + Y _ { i } Y _ { i + 1 } + Z _ { i } Z _ { i + 1 } ) . \quad ( 1 3 )
$$

The initial state is the zero state, ρ = |0⟩⟨0|⊗n , and Eα(ρ) is a variational ansatz with m = 3nL parameters composed of L-layers of single-qubit rotations followed by two-qubit ZZ gates of the form

$$
U ( \alpha ) = \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { L } \left ( \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } R _ { Z Z } ( \alpha _ { i + 2 n , l } ) \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } R _ { Z } ( \alpha _ { i + n , l } ) R _ { X } ( \alpha _ { i , l } ) \right ) \, . \quad \begin{array} { c c } \text {number} & \text {numb} \\ \text {of Eq.} & \text {describ} \end{array} \\ \text {We compute the sum of the $\nabla \tilde{f}(\alpha)$ of ground} \\ \text {We compute the sum of the $\nabla \tilde{f}(\alpha)$ of ground} \\
$$

We compute the surrogate of the gradient ∇ f̃(α) around the randomly chosen initial point α∗ and use it to train the algorithm until f(α), the test loss, stops decreasing. Let α0 denote the corresponding parameters at that step. Finally, we compute ∥α∗ - α0∥x, where x ∈ {∞, 2, 1}, with respect to the number of parameters in the circuit

m.The results are presented in Fig. 3, where the diamonds are the numerical values of ∥α∗ -α0∥x for x = 1, 2,∞ after averaging for 20 runs with different initial points (with the maximum and minimum shaded), the solid lines are a fit of those points and the dashed lines represent the scalings we would expect from the bound. Namely, we recall that according to Theorem 1, ∥α∗ -α0∥∞ ∈ O(m-0.5 ), ∥α∗ -α0∥2 ∈ O(1), ∥α∗ -α0∥1 ∈ O(m0.5 ), compared to the ∥α∗ -α0∥∞ ∈ O(m-0.15 ), ∥α∗ -α0∥2 ∈ O(m0.2 ), ∥α∗ - α0∥1 ∈ O(m0.7 ), found in the experiment. We thus see that the experimental results have, roughly, between a m0.2 and m0.3 better scaling (i.e., the solid lines are angled above the dashed lines). This implies that the optimization process can use the surrogate for significantly longer distances than our bounds predict.

In Appendix D, we further provide a preliminary comparison between the quantum resources required for an iterative surrogate approach as compared to the standard hybrid quantum classical approach. As there are many different hyper-parameters for this benchmarking problem, it is hard to fairly draw definitive conclusions. In particular, the standard gradient-descent approach is operationally simpler, as it requires tuning fewer hyperparameters than the iterative surrogate strategy, which additionally introduces choices such as the frequency of the surrogate reconstruction. Moreover, gradients estimated directly on quantum hardware are unbiased, rendering the method more resilient to shot noise. Nevertheless, our numerics suggest that the bias introduced by the Taylor approximation can, in some regimes, be balanced by the increased statistical noise associated with repeated gradient estimation in the standard approach, although we do not claim this as a general result.

As such, we present both cases where the surrogate outperforms a standard variational quantum algorithm and vice versa. We therefore suggest that this Taylor surrogate should be viewed as a potential alternative to vanilla VQAs, rather than a replacement, with potential practical advantages given the problems associated with noise drift and/or queuing for cloud access.

## IV. NEAR-CLIFFORD PATCHES

We will now specialise to particular families of problems for which the requirements of the quantum computer can be substantially reduced as compared to the Taylor surrogate approach. In particular, in this section, we consider a patch of a quantum landscape that is centered around a Clifford circuit. We utilize the Pauli propagation approach described in Section IIB with a truncation method specialized for this near-Clifford region.

## A. Error guarantees

Concretely, we consider the circuit composed of alternating layers of Clifford gates and uncorrelated Pauli rotations defined in Eq. (5). We then consider a hypercube of width 2r around the all zero vector 0. In this region, the angles αi are small and so we can leverage the fact that sine contributions to the coefficients cP in Eq. (8) are much smaller than cosine contributions. We use this observation to prune Pauli paths with many sine coefficients, similar to the approach in Ref. [50]. Then, we show that this 'small-angle' truncation strategy achieves an exponentially decreasing average error as a function of the sine expansion order κ (i.e., the number of sine terms kept).

## 1. Classical time complexity

We begin by presenting a theorem (derived in Appendix E5), which studies the time complexity of the classical simulation algorithm.

Theorem 2 (Time complexity of small-angle Pauli propagation, Informal). Consider an expectation function f(α) of the form of Eq. (1) with an observable O = ∑P∈Pn aPP, where at most NPaulis ∈ O(poly(n)) coefficients aP are non zero, with ∥a∥1 ∈ O(1), and a parametrized circuit of the form of Eq. (5) with m independent parameters. If the expectation values Tr[ρP] of the initial state ρ with any Pauli operator P can be efficiently computed, a runtime of

$$
t \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { P a u l i s } \cdot m ^ { \log \left ( \frac { 1 } { c ^ { 2 } \delta } \right ) } \right ) \, , \quad \text { (15)} \quad \complement \, \text {scale}
$$

suffices to classically simulate f(α) up to an error ϵ with probability at least 1- δ for random α ∼Dm(0, r) with

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { m } } \right ) .
$$

Similarly, with a runtime of

$$
t \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { P a u l i s } \cdot m ^ { \log \left ( \frac { 1 } { \epsilon } \right ) } \right ) \, .
$$

we can classically simulate f(α) up to an error ϵ for all α in the hypercube Vm(0, r) with

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { m } \right )
$$

A more formal version of the theorem is stated in Supplemental Theorems 6 and 7.

Thus we see that Pauli propagation can be used to efficiently simulate Pauli expectation values for arbitrary circuits of the form Eq. (5) with high probability for all α in a hypercube of width O(1/√m) and for all α in a smaller hypercube of width O(1/m). Notice that these results mirror the general result in Theorem 1, but for a family of circuits that is itself classically tractable. It follows that as long as we have a classical representation of the initial state ρ (analytically or, e.g., as a tensor network) from which we can efficiently compute expectation values of Paulis, then it is possible to classically simulate VQAs with high probability in the small angle regimes for which trainability guarantees have been derived [33, 34, 36, 37]. Moreover, our numerics in Section IVB suggest that the above bounds are somewhat loose and improved resource scalings can be obtained in practice.

In Appendix E4, we further provide a bound for the average error of an observable with a circuit where all angles are correlated (i.e., where all the rotation gates are parametrized by the same α). Intriguingly, we find that the average-case simulation of such maximally correlated angles is asymptotically as hard as the worst-case as a function of m. It is not clear whether the equal scaling is mostly due to our proof techniques, or whether simulating cases such as real-time dynamics of quantum systems is about as hard as the worst-case.

An interesting corollary of our worst-case analysis is that we can provably efficiently simulate quantum dynamics of Hamiltonians with O(n) Pauli terms and O(1)- sized coefficients up to time t = O ( 1n ) on any geometry. This is because, for L Trotter layers with time step dt, we have m = Θ(nL) parameters and dt = O ( 1m ) is the largest guaranteed time step. We thus have dt = O ( 1 nL ), and with t = Ldt we find t = O ( 1n ) is guaranteed to be efficient.

Given our significantly more favorable average-case errors for uncorrelated angles it stands to reason that any independence of parameters interpolates between our two complexity bounds. In Section IVC we present a largescale (127 qubits) numerical demonstration of Pauli propagation for a quantum simulation problem with a timedependent Hamiltonian which supports this hypothesis.

## 2. Sample complexity

In the previous section, we assumed that we could efficiently estimate the overlap terms Tr[ρP] in Eq. (8). If these can be efficiently computed classically for any Pauli operator P, then the time complexity computed in the previous section fully quantifies the complexity of our small-angle patch simulation. If, instead, we do not have a classical representation of the state ρ, then we need a quantum device to measure the expectation value Tr[ρP ]. Here we quantify the number of measurements required, i.e., the sample complexity, in the case where ρ is nonclassically simulable. As we will see, this sampling step is efficient in the sense that it requires only a polynomial number of Pauli measurements on quantum hardware.

There are several different tomographic methods that could be used to measure the necessary Tr[ρP ]. The simplest is to take the total shot budget Ns and divide them equally between each of the Pauli terms that need to be measured. In this case, analogously to Theorem 2, the number of terms that need to be measured scales polynomially with the number of parameters for a hypercube region with r ∈ O(1/√m). More concretely, the following theorem holds.

Theorem 3 (Surrogation via Direct Pauli Measurements, Informal). Consider an expectation f(α) of the form of Eq. (1) with an observable O = ∑P∈Pn aPP containing at most NPaulis ∈ O(poly(n)) Pauli terms with ∥a∥1 ∈ O(1) and a parametrized circuit of the form of Eq. (5) with m independent parameters. A shot budget of

$$
N _ { s } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { P a u l i s } \ m ^ { \log ( \frac { 1 } { \epsilon ^ { 2 } \delta } ) } \right ) \\
$$

suffices to classically surrogate f(α) with an error ∆f(α) of at most ϵ with probability at least 1-δ for any random α ∼ Dm(0, r).

A more formal version of the theorem together with the proof is provided in Appendix E6d in the form of Supplemental Theorem 12.

While the above strategy is simple, in general using the same number of shots to measure each of the Pauli terms is suboptimal. Indeed, if some of the Paulis have larger coefficients than others (on average over the patch of interest) it is advantageous to use more shots to measure the terms that contribute more to the final expectation value (see warm-up example in Appendix B1 comparing different shot allocation strategies). In fact, it is precisely the exponentially decreasing contribution per sineexpansion order that we leverage for our efficient simulation guarantees. Hence, one should allocate the shots to each term in proportion to the average coefficient of that Pauli over the patch. In this case, the sample complexity becomes effectively independent of NPaulis and instead scales with the 1-norm of the averaged coefficients. That is, we show that the number of shots scales as

$$
N _ { s } \in \mathcal { O } ( \| c \| _ { 1 , a v g } ^ { 2 } ) \, , & & ( 2 0 ) & & \quad
$$

where we call ∥c∥1,avg := ∑P∈Pn √Eα∼D[cP (α)2 ] an "effective" 1-norm. We describe this quantity in more detail in Appendix B2 and Appendix E6a. The surrogation guarantees with the effective 1-norm allocation strategy are further provided in Appendix E 6 b (averagecase) and Appendix E6 c (worst-case).

We note that a similar strategy can be employed if one is not interested in surrogating a whole patch but rather evaluating a single expectation value for a given α0 without running U(α0) on the quantum computer. This is often true in the context of dynamical simulation, where one seeks f(α) at only a handful of parameter values. In this case, by allocating the total shot budget to Pauli P in proportion |aP | one obtains a sample complexity that scales as Ns ∝ ∥a∥21 .

Another popular family of tomographic procedures are classical shadows [91-93]. Their variant for Pauli expectation values, which we here call Pauli classical shadows, enjoy tight accuracy guarantees but require more restricted families of circuits for optimal efficiency. Here we suppose that the Clifford layers consist exclusively of single-qubit Clifford gates and the Pauli rotation layers are O(1)-local. Now, each of the back-propagated Pauli operators have Pauli weight at most O(κ), where κ is the sine-truncation order. Thus, for κ ∈ O(log(n)), i.e., the regime in which small-angle Pauli Propagation enjoys polynomial run times, we can employ classical shadows. This is captured by the following theorem.

Theorem 4 (Shadow Surrogation Guarantee, Informal). Consider an expectation function f(α) of the form of Eq. (1), an observable O = ∑P∈Pn aPP composed of at most NPaulis ∈ O(poly(n)) Pauli terms with ∥a∥1 ∈ O(1), and a parameterized quantum circuit of the form of Eq. (5) with the additional constraints that Ci are singlequbit Clifford operations and the Pauli rotations Ui(αi) act on O(1) qubits. A shot budget of

$$
N _ { s } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { \min \left ( \log ( n ) , \log ( m \, N _ { P a u l i s } ) \right ) } { \epsilon } \right ) , \quad ( 2 1 )
$$

random Pauli measurements suffices to achieve a mean square error at most ϵ with high probability over a uniformly sampled hypercube Dm(0, r) with r ∈ O ( 1 √m ).

A more formal version of this theorem is provided in Appendix E 6 b as Supplemental Theorem 10. This tomographic procedure is well-studied and follows a "measure first, ask questions later" philosophy. Thus, one might wonder how this shot allocation strategy fairs against the seemingly more informed effective 1-norm allocation described above. The general scalings in Appendix E6b do not allow for a clear comparison between the approaches, but we compare them numerically in Fig. 4. While circuits with multi-qubit Clifford gates would likely make Pauli classical shadows underperform, there is a significant upside in having collected a more general set of measurements that can be used to estimate other quantities with a similar error.

> *Generated caption of `2411.19896_-_Efficient_quantum-enhanced_classical_simulation_for_patches_of_quantum_landscapes_assets/figure-004.png` (ollama:gemma4:26b, prompt sci-v1):* This image contains three line plots, labeled a), b), and c), showing the relationship between error and resources. Plot a) has the y-axis labeled "RMSE" on a logarithmic scale ranging from $10^{-6}$ to $10^0$ and the x-axis labeled "Number of Pauli Operators (Time on Classical Hardware)" on a logarithmic scale from $10^0$ to $10^4$. Multiple data series are shown with different colors and markers: a dark blue line with dots ($r=1.0$), a medium blue line with dots ($r=0.5$), a light blue line with dots ($r=0.3$), a purple line with dots ($r=0.1$), and a light blue line with dots ($r=0.01$), all showing a downward trend as the number of operators increases. A vertical gray line is present at $10^2$ with an arrow pointing to it labeled "Expansion order $\kappa = 6$". Plot b) has the y-axis labeled "RMSE" on a logarithmic scale from $10^{-6}$ to $10^0$ and the x-axis labeled "Number of Measurements (Time on Quantum Hardware)" on a logarithmic scale from $10^2$ to $10^7$. The same five data series from plot a) are repeated here, showing a downward trend as the number of measurements increases. Plot c) has the y-axis labeled "RMSE" on a logarithmic scale from $10^{-6}$ to $10^0$ and the x-axis labeled "Number of Measurements" on a logarithmic scale from $10^2$ to $10^6$. It features a legend with three entries: "Uniform Allocation" (a pink line with circles), "Pauli Shadows" (a dark red line with diamonds), and "1-norm Allocation" (a light pink line with circles). All three series show a downward trend as the number of measurements increases, with the "Pauli Shadows" series generally staying above the others.

## B. Numerical resource and error scalings

In this section, we present numerical experiments that indicate that both the time complexity and error of Pauli Propagation methods equipped with small-angle truncation outperforms the guarantees provided by our bounds.

First we test the error incurred by the truncated Pauli propagation together and the shot noise in an exactly verifiable system. We focus on the case of the Hamiltonian Variational Ansatz (HVA) [94] for which reduced parameter range trainability guarantees were derived in Ref. [34]. This HVA family of circuits has the structure of the Trotterized time evolution under the Hamiltonian, but with mostly, or fully, free rotation angles. Here we choose a transverse field Ising (TFI) Hamiltonian

$$
H _ { \text {TFI} } = H _ { X } + H _ { Z Z } & & ( 2 2 ) & \ t i o { 2 }
$$

with

$$
H _ { X } = - \sum _ { i } h _ { i } X _ { i } \, , \ H _ { Z Z } = - \sum _ { \langle i , j \rangle } J _ { i j } Z _ { i } Z _ { j } \, , \quad \text { (23)} \quad \text {shot} \quad \ t a i n t i n
$$

where X,Z are Pauli operators, and hi, Jij coefficients. The topology of the ZZ-interaction terms is that of nearest neighbors on a 4x4 grid in a 16-qubit system.

To demonstrate the freedom in our approach, we choose the initial state ρ as the exactly time-evolved allzero state for 4 Trotter layers with hj = Jij = 1 and dt = 0.1, and we surrogate the final 4 layers with the same HVA circuit ansatz but with independent parameters. The observable is a Z observable in the middle of the grid.

In Fig. 4a), we show the root mean square error (RMSE) of the truncated simulation as a function of the number of Pauli operators computed at increasing sine-truncation order κ, and for different angle ranges r. For example, the RMSE for r = 0.1 goes below 10-6 at expansion order κ = 6, which computes around 102 Pauli operators. As a reference, the corresponding classical surrogation procedure takes a few milliseconds on a standard laptop with expectation evaluations being significantly faster. If the initial state is classically simulable this time complexity effectively quantifies the total simulation cost. If not, then we additionally need to account for the effect of shot noise.

Moving onto the quantum resource requirements for non-classically simulable initial states, in Fig. 4b), we record the total error of the expectation value as a function of the number of measurements of ρ. We allocate the measurements according to the effective 1-norm allocation described before. The numerical results seem to indicate that the total error for r ⩽ 0.5 is dominated by shot noise even with 107 measurements, as those uncertainties are orders of magnitudes larger than the classical approximation error for small r. This argument can be exemplified by the extreme case of r = 0, where a single operator perfectly reconstructs the "landscape". Yet, the total error scales as ∼ 1 √Ns , where Ns is the number of measurements.

In Fig. 4c), we compare the effective 1-norm allocation to uniform allocation over all operators and to Pauli classical shadows. While uniform allocation understandably underperforms, Pauli classical shadows and the effective 1-norm allocation are on-par for this example. However, the setup considered here is among the best-case scenarios for Pauli classical shadows, with more global observables or patches around non-Identity unitaries deteriorating its relative performance.

These results are complemented by the plots shown in Fig. 9 in Appendix E7. There we showcase a Heisenberg model HVA circuit structure instead of the TFI HVA in Fig. 4. The number of generated Pauli strings is larger, which, broadly speaking, increases the surrogation and measurement errors at the same classical or quantum resources, respectively. Due to the increased structure and number of commuting, overlapping terms in the Heisenberg Hamiltonian, the 1-norm estimation strategy underperforms without additional grouping of simultaneously measurable terms. How to best pair commuting observables and allocate quantum computing resources for the simulation of quantum landscape patches remains an open problem for future work.

## C. Large-scale small-angle Pauli Propagation

In this section, we numerically demonstrate smallangle Pauli propagation on a large-scale problem. When picking a system to showcase the algorithm, there is a fine balance to be struck between picking a complex problem that is beyond the realm of exact simulation methods and yet one where we can be confident in the results obtained. To address this tension we will draw motivation from Ref. [95], where the authors used analytically known scalings for benchmarking. While Ref. [95] takes this approach to benchmark a quantum simulation on a quantum device, we here apply the same logic to benchmark our approximate classical simulation at scale.

The system we consider is a time-dependent transverse field Ising (TFI) model with 127 spins and on a heavy-hex entangling topology [1]. The time-dependent TFI Hamiltonian is related to the time-independent TFI Hamiltonian in Eq. (22) and reads

$$
H ( t ) = ( 1 - g ( t ) ) H _ { X } + g ( t ) H _ { Z Z } \, . \quad ( 2 4 ) \quad \text {harov}
$$

As before, we set the coefficients in HX and HZZ (see Eq. (23)) to hi = Jij = 1. Importantly, the ramp function g(t) is chosen such that it interpolates between the two Hamiltonians HX and HZZ from time t = 0 to the final time t = tf , i.e., g(0) = 0, g(tf) = 1. Given the initial state ρ = |+⟩⟨+|⊗n , the evolution begins in the ground state of the transverse Hamiltonian. This initial state and other stabilizer states like the all-zero state |0⟩⟨0| allow for very fast and exact evaluation of the final overlaps Tr[ρP] for any Pauli operator P.

The phenomenon we leverage to benchmark our classical surrogate algorithm is the Kibble-Zurek (KZ) mechanism [96-98]. It predicts the density of defects in the evolved state relative to the ground state of the final Hamiltonian (here HZZ) as the system crosses a critical point with a finite annealing time tf . Defects here are detected by non-zero values for quantities like 1-⟨ZiZj⟩

between any two sites i, j. In the limit of infinitely slowly ramping between HX and HZZ, the state remains in the instantaneous ground state and there will be no defects. For a 1D TFI model, the density of defects ndef has been shown to scale as ndef ∝ t-1/2 f . Interestingly, the authors in Ref. [95] conjecture that experiments on the heavy-hex topology should follow a very similar scaling because the topology is closer to 1D than 2D.

Here we provide numerical evidence for this conjecture by simulating the time evolution of the time-dependent TFI model in Eq. (24) with 127 spins on a heavy-hex topology. We use a smaller time step of dt = 0.3, use the full heavy-hex lattice, and simulate to significantly longer times than possible with the quantum device in Ref. [95]. In this simulation, we use a combination of weight truncation [26] and our sine-expansion order truncation. Concretely, we truncate Pauli operators with Pauli weight of W > 5 (i.e., more than 5 non-identity Paulis) and with a number of sines κ > 21. Constructing the classical surrogate for the Z62Z63 Pauli operator in the middle of the lattice with 50 Trotter layers took under 30 seconds using one CPU thread on a laptop. We then record the density of defects on these lattice sites via ndef = 1-⟨Z62Z63⟩ on the surrogate landscape at points corresponding to different final evolution times and annealing ramps. This took under one second per evaluation.

Our results can be seen in Fig. 5. The linear ramp is defined via g(t) = t/tf , the square ramp via g(t) = (t/tf)2 , and the tanh ramp via g(t) = 1 2 [tanh(-3 (1- t tf ) +3 t tf ) + 1] which sweeps over the center part of a rescaled tanh function. Not only do we observe that all three annealing ramps exhibit the analytically known scaling of the defect density, but we can verify the quality of our results by observing that the norm of the Heisenberg-evolved observable is very high between 84% and 99% its original 2-norm after 50 circuit layers. Thus we see that our Pauli propagation simulation algorithm vastly exceeds its theoretical guarantees and, arguably, the capabilities of most current quantum hardware.

It is evident that the classical simulations could be pushed significantly further, for example, to attempt exploring when the tree-like scaling on the heavy-hex geometry transitions into a 2D scaling. This is coincidentally the regime where the classical simulation should become substantially harder. Our work merely highlights that PP surrogates with their fast re-evaluation capabilities can be used to explore entire families of quantum protocols, particularly using our sine-truncation in the smallangle regime, for later deployment on quantum devices.

## V. DISCUSSION

This work described classically surrogating a subregion (dubbed a "patch") of an expectation landscape produced by a parameterized quantum circuit. The surrogation procedure may require quantum resources but subsequent evaluations can be performed fully classically. This alleviates the load of quantum computers in a wide range of quantum algorithms, like VQAs, quantum machine learning, dynamical simulation, and even quantum sensing, by greatly reducing the necessary gate count and the number of measurements.

> *Generated caption of `2411.19896_-_Efficient_quantum-enhanced_classical_simulation_for_patches_of_quantum_landscapes_assets/figure-005.png` (ollama:gemma4:26b, prompt sci-v1):* A line plot shows the relationship between $n_{\text{def}}$ on the vertical y-axis and $t_f$ on the horizontal x-axis, both on logarithmic scales. The y-axis ranges from $0.25$ to $1.0$ and the x-axis ranges from $1$ to $15$. The plot contains four data series: a solid black line labeled $\propto t_f^{-1/2}$, a blue line with square markers labeled "Linear Ramp", a light blue line with square markers labeled "Squared Ramp", and a gray line with downward-pointing triangle markers labeled "Tanh Ramp". All series exhibit a downward trend as $t_f$ increases. The "Tanh Ramp" series stays above the other three, the "Squared Ramp" series is below the "Tanh Ramp" but above the "Linear Ramp", and the "Linear Ramp" series follows a trend nearly parallel to and slightly below the $\propto t_f^{-1/2}$ reference line. All data points for the ramps start near $n_{\text{def}} = 1.0$ at $t_f = 1$.

We presented three main scenarios demanding progressively more modest quantum resources. Section III considered arbitrarily located patches in very general circuits acting on potentially unknown (but quantumly preparable) initial states. Here, the surrogation prescribes executing the deepest (inside this work) yet relatively undemanding, non-adaptive circuits and measurements. Section IV considered patches centered around parameters for which the circuit is Clifford. This simplifies the task by enabling efficient classical simulation of the quantum circuit with our Pauli propagation algorithm. In this case, for states that cannot be classically simulated, but can be prepared on quantum hardware, our surrogates require only polynomially few Pauli measurements upon the initial state. And for classically tractable initial states ρ, whereby Tr[ρP] with a Pauli string P is efficiently classically evaluable, the surrogate can now be computed entirely classically.

We highlight that our scalings for efficient surrogatability and simulation align with prior work on the absence of barren plateau guarantees inside patches of width O(1/√m) [33-39]. Thus our findings here complement the conjecture connecting the provable absence of barren plateaus and efficient classical surrogatability [8].

More positively, however, we have expanded the corpus of quantum-enhanced classical algorithms, consequently expanding the collection of problems that can be tackled with both classical and quantum resources. These hybrid approaches are emerging as a notable intermediary between fully classical and quantum settings, thus nuancing the threshold of practical quantum advantage [99, 100]. In particular, classical surrogation of a patch can augment a wide class of quantum algorithms, addressing shortcomings like that of insufficient coherence times and gate fidelities to reliably prepare ansatz states, but which can faithfully prepare and measure initial states. As quantum hardware improves, computations could be linked up in the middle of the quantum circuit, as we have shown in Fig. 4, which in turn reduces the classical complexity. It may also alleviate the need for experimentally difficult parametrized unitaries that can be more straightforwardly applied classically. The surrogated patch also has utility as a symbolic approximation to the parametrized landscape. In addition to being evaluated at particular points, it can be analytically studied and ergo integrated with, for example, symbolic and global minimization techniques [101], as has been shown to improve VQA convergence [102].

In general, we have shown that quantum preparation of a classical surrogate is often efficient and can be used to simplify an algorithm's prescribed quantum operations. We caution however that we have not shown this process is necessarily faster than the original quantum algorithm running on compatible hardware. Constants and prefactors may well mean, for example, that surrogation of a variational cost function can involve more total measurements and operations than direct quantum evaluation. This is indeed supported by our numerics in Appendix D where we find that Taylor surrogation outperforms our bounds, and may well have practical advantages, but has a broadly compatible performance to vanilla variational quantum algorithms. Even in the case of near-Clifford patches where the quantum resources required to surrogate are minimal, there could also end up being polynomial speed ups from running directly on quantum hardware when the initial state is quantum. Still, by adding these hybrid methods to our algorithmic arsenal and analyzing their performance, we hope to have patched a hole in the community understanding of what is classically simulable, surrogatable, and worthwhile deploying to quantum computers.

## Appendix A: Preliminaries

## 1. Notation

| General notation table - Symbol   | General notation table - Definition                                                                                                  |
|-----------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| n                                 | Number of qubits                                                                                                                     |
| α                                 | A vector of variational real parameters and its ith component                                                                        |
| αi                                | ith component of α                                                                                                                   |
| m                                 | Number of variational parameters i.e., the size of α                                                                                 |
| f (α)                             | The expectation value Tr[OEα(ρ)] with an observable O, an initial state ρ and a parametrized quantum channel Eα(·)                   |
| f (α)                             | Approximate function that can be efficiently implemented.                                                                            |
| ∆f (α)                            | Difference between an approximate function and the original function |f(α) - f(α)| evaluated at α                          |
| Vm(α∗ , r)                        | Hypercube of width 2r around some fixed point α∗ in the parameter space, Vm(α∗ , r) := {α} such that αi ∈ [α∗ i - r, α∗ i + r] , ∀ i |
| Dm(α∗ , r)                        | Uniform distribution of α over Vm(α∗ , r) also referred to as Unif[Vm(α∗ , r)]                                                       |
| f̃ (α)                           | Surrogate of f(α).                                                                                                                   |
| ̂ f (α)                           | Empirical etimator of f(α).                                                                                                          |
| ̂f̃ (α)                          | Empirical estimator of the surrogate.                                                                                                |
| f (P ) (α)                        | The subscript (P) refers to f (P) (α) = Tr[ρU † (α)PU(α)].                                                                           |
| κ                                 | Order of expansion of a surrogate f̃(α) of f(α).                                                                                    |
| Ns                                | Number of measurements.                                                                                                              |
| ô                               | Empirical estimate of an observable with a state Tr[ρO].                                                                             |
| ô(α)                            | Empirical estimate of a back-propagated observable with a state Tr[ρO(α)].                                                           |
| S                                 | Set of "experimentally friendly" observables. A typical choice is a set of Pauli i.e. S ⊆ Pn.                                        |
| Λ                                 | Upper-bound on the infinity norm of all the elements in S, i.e. Λ ⩾ ∥P∥∞ ∀P ∈ S.                                                     |
| β(·)                              | β : S →]0, 1] such that ∑P∈S β(P) = 1 and β(P) > 0 ∀P ∈ S.                                                                           |
