## 2. Concentration of measure

We recall some useful tail bounds which we will employ throughout the paper. For further details, see also [103] and references therein.

Lemma 1 (Markov's inequality). Let X be a positive random variable (i.e. X ⩾ 0) and ϵ > 0. Then, we have

$$
\Pr [ X \geq \epsilon ] \leqslant \frac { \mathbb { E } [ X ] } { \epsilon }
$$

Lemma 2 (Hoeffding's inequality [104]). Given ϵ, δ ∈ (0, 1), c > 0, let Ns = log(2/δ)c2/(2ϵ2 ) Suppose X1,X2, . . . ,XNs are independent random variables taking values in [a, b] such that |a- b| ⩽ c . Let X denote their mean. Then for any ϵ > 0,

$$
\Pr [ | X - \mathbb { E } [ X ] | \geqslant \epsilon ] \leqslant \delta .
$$

## 3. Central limit theorem and moments of a Normal distribution

We present and explain the Central Limit theorem and discuss the moments of a random variable that follows a normal distribution. For more information we refer the reader to Ref. [105]

Supplemental Theorem 1 (Lindeberg-Lévy: Central Limit theorem). Suppose that {αi}mi=1 is an independent sequence of m random variables having the same distribution with mean E[αi] = µ and finite positive variance Var[αi] = σ2 1 . If Sm = ∑mi=1 αi, then

$$
X _ { m } = \frac { S _ { m } - m \mu } { \sigma _ { 1 } \sqrt { m } } \underset { m \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } \mathcal { N } ( 0 , 1 )
$$

i.e. the random variable Xm tends to a normal distribution with mean µN = 0, and variance σN = 1.

Using Supplemental Theorem 1 for a sequence of random variables {αi}mi=1 with E[αi] = 0 we get that the probability distribution of the random variable Xm tends to behave as a normal distribution with variance 1 and mean 0, or in other words

$$
X _ { m } = \frac { S _ { m } } { \sigma _ { m } } \underset { m \to \infty } { \longrightarrow } \mathcal { N } ( 0 , 1 )
$$

where we have used that σ1√m= σm as all the αi are independent and have the same mean.

The k-th moment of such distribution is defined as the expected value of the variable to the k-th power, i.e. E[Xkm]. This can be computed as follows [106]

$$
\mathbb { E } [ X _ { m } ^ { k } ] = \int _ { - \infty } ^ { \infty } X _ { m } ^ { k } \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { 2 \pi } } e ^ { - \frac { X _ { m } ^ { 2 } } { 2 } } = \begin{cases} 0 \text { if odd} \\ ( k - 1 ) ! \text { if even} \end{cases}
$$

where a!! is the double factorial defined for a ∈ N as a!!(a-1)!! = a!, or in other words a!! = a(a-2)(a-4)(a-6)...1.

## 4. Binomial inequalities

In subsequent sections, we will make frequent use of the following binomial inequalities:

$$
\left ( \frac { m } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \leqslant \left ( \begin{matrix} m \\ k \end{matrix} \right ) \leqslant \frac { m ^ { k } } { k ! } < \left ( \frac { e m } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \, ,
$$

where we assume 0 ⩽ k ⩽m and e is the Euler number. In fact, the rightmost inequality is loose enough so that for k ⩽ m we have

$$
\sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k } \binom { m } { j } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m } { k } \right ) ^ { k } .
$$

We also prove the following more general lemmas.

Lemma 3. For any x ∈ [0, 1] such that k ⩽mx, we have

$$
\sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k } \binom { m } { j } x ^ { j } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m x } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \, .
$$

$$
\sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k } \binom { m } { j } x ^ { j } \leqslant \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k } \frac { ( m x ) ^ { j } } { j ! } = \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k } \left ( \frac { m x } { k } \right ) ^ { j } \frac { k ^ { j } } { j ! } \leqslant \left ( \frac { m x } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { k } \frac { k ^ { j } } { j ! } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m x } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \, ,
$$

where we first use Eq. (A6). The second inequality is obtained from the condition k ⩽ xm i.e. ( xm k )j increases with j so reaches its maximum for j = k. The last inequality is obtained by adding terms in the sum (up to j =∞) and recognising the exponential series.

Lemma 4. For any x such that x ⩽ k/m we have

$$
\sum _ { j = k } ^ { m } \binom { m } { j } x ^ { j } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m x } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \, .
$$

Proof. The proof of Eq. (A10) is similar to Eq. (A8) and reads as follows

$$
\sum _ { j = k } ^ { m } \binom { m } { j } x ^ { j } \leqslant \sum _ { j = k } ^ { m } x ^ { j } \frac { m ^ { j } } { j ! } = \sum _ { j = k } ^ { m } \left ( \frac { x m } { k } \right ) ^ { j } \frac { k ^ { j } } { j ! } \leqslant \left ( \frac { x m } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \sum _ { j = k } ^ { m } \frac { k ^ { j } } { j ! } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m x } { k } \right ) ^ { k } \, ,
$$

where we first use Eq. (A6). The second inequality is obtained from the condition xm⩽ k i.e. ( xm k )j decreases with j so reaches a maximum for j = k. The last inequality is obtained by adding terms in the sum (from j = 0 to j =∞) and recognizing the exponential series.

Proof.

6. Discard λ(sol) .
7. The points x(sol) are the solution to the problem.

## 6. Taylor remainder theorem

We now introduce the Taylor Remainder Theorem, which will be used below to bound the error arising from making a k-order expansion in the expectation function. For further details on the Taylor reminder theorem we refer the reader to Ref. [108].

Supplemental Theorem 2 (Taylor Reminder Theorem). Consider a multivariate differentiable function f(x) such that f : Rm → R and some positive integer κ. The function f(x) can be expanded around some fixed point x∗ ∈ Rm as

$$
f ( x ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( x ^ { * } ) \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( x , x ^ { * } ) + \epsilon _ { \kappa , x ^ { * } } ( x ) \ ,
$$

$$
\partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( x ^ { * } ) & \coloneqq \left ( \frac { \partial ^ { k } f ( x ) } { \partial x _ { i _ { 1 } } \partial x _ { i _ { 2 } } \dots \partial x _ { i _ { k } } } \right ) \Big | _ { x \colon = x ^ { * } } \, , \\ \\ \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( x ^ { * } ) & \coloneqq \left ( \frac { \eta _ { * } } { \partial x _ { i _ { 1 } } \partial x _ { i _ { 2 } } \dots \partial x _ { i _ { k } } } \right ) \Big | _ { x \colon = x ^ { * } } \, ,
$$

$$
\Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( x , x ^ { * } ) = ( x _ { i _ { 1 } } - x _ { i _ { 1 } } ^ { * } ) ( x _ { i _ { 2 } } - x _ { i _ { 2 } } ^ { * } ) \dots ( x _ { i _ { k } } - x _ { i _ { k } } ^ { * } ) \, .
$$

Then, the remainder of this function ϵκ,x∗(x) can be expressed as follows

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , x ^ { * } } ( x ) = \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { 1 } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } f ( \tilde { x } ) \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } ( x , x ^ { * } ) \, ,
$$

with x̃ = τx+ (1- τ)x∗ for some τ ∈ [0, 1].

## Appendix B: Shot noise analysis for estimating observables with restricted measurements

In this section we analyse the effect of shot noise for different strategies of computing families of observables. We will use these results in the later appendices to compute the effect of shot noise for our different surrogation strategies.

Given an observable O and a quantum state ρ, we consider the task of estimating the expectation values Tr[ρO] with quantum experiments. In many practical settings, a direct measurement of the observable O on the state ρ

where we are denoting

## 5. Lagrange multipliers

In this section we present the method of Lagrange multipliers to find the minima of a function subjected to some constraints. We refer the reader to Ref. [107]. The optimisation algorithm works as follows.

## Lagrange multipliers method: optimisation algorithm.

1. Let f(x) to be the function to optimise.
2. Let g(x) = 0 to be the constrain function.
3. Define the Lagrangian: L(x, λ) = f(x) + λg(x).
4. Compute the derivatives: ∂ ∂xi L(x, λ) and ∂ ∂λ L(x, λ).
5. Solve the following system of equations

$$
\begin{cases} \frac { \partial } { \partial x _ { i } } \mathcal { L } ( x , \lambda ) = 0 \, , \\ \frac { \partial } { \partial \lambda } \mathcal { L } ( x , \lambda ) = 0 \, . \end{cases}
$$

might be experimentally unfeasible. Thus, we define a set S of "experimentally friendly" observables such that we can decompose O as

$$
O = \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } a _ { P } P ,
$$

where |S| is of a manageable size and each P is easily measurable. A typical choice of S is a subset of the Pauli basis, i.e. Pn := {I,X, Y,Z}⊗n . While in Sections C7 and E we will precise consider such basis, we here make no assumption, and leave S as a general set of observables. By measuring each P ∈ S individually, we can estimate an approximate value of Tr[Oρ]. We start by providing a general measurement protocol for estimating Tr[ρO] up to a desired error ϵ.

Lemma 5 (Parametric estimator with restricted measurements). Let O = ∑P∈S aPP, where aP are non-zero real coefficients and S is a set of observables, for which each element can be measured efficiently. Moreover, assume that ∥P∥∞ ⩽ Λ for all P ∈ S. Let β : S →]0, 1] be a probability distribution whose support is S (i.e., β(·) satisfies ∑P∈S β(P) = 1 and β(P) > 0 ∀P ∈ S).

1. There exists a randomized measurement protocol which uses Λ2/ϵ2 measurements on different copies of an unknown state ρ, to output an estimate ô satisfying

$$
\sqrt { \mathbb { E } [ ( \hat { o } - T r [ O \rho ] ) ^ { 2 } ] } < \epsilon \sqrt { \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { a _ { P } ^ { 2 } } { \beta ( P ) } } .
$$

2. There exists a probabilistic measurement protocol which uses 2Λ2 log(2/δ)/ϵ2 measurements on different copies of an unknown state ρ, to output an estimate ô satisfying

$$
| \hat { o } - \text {Tr} [ O \rho ] | < \epsilon \cdot \max _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { | a _ { P } | } { \beta ( P ) } ,
$$

with probability at least 1- δ.

Proof. Given Ns ⩾ 1, for i = 1, 2, . . . ,Ns, we perform the following steps:

1. Sample an operator Pi ∈ S with probability β(Pi) .
2. Measure Pi on (an unused copy of) the state ρ .
3. Denote the measurement outcome as x̂i .
4. Define the random variable

We also note that

$$
\widehat { X } _ { i } = \frac { a _ { P _ { i } } } { \beta ( P _ { i } ) } \hat { x } _ { i } .
$$

We notice that ̂Xi is an unbiased estimator for Tr[ρO]:

$$
\mathbb { E } \widehat { X } _ { i } = \sum _ { P \in S } \beta ( P ) \frac { a _ { P } } { \beta ( P ) } \, \text {Tr} [ P \rho ] = \text {Tr} [ O \rho ] .
$$

$$
\left | \widehat { X } _ { i } \right | \leqslant \Lambda \max _ { P \in S } \frac { | a _ { P } | } { \beta ( P ) } ,
$$

and therefore ̂ Xi takes value in an interval of size at most 2ΛmaxP∈S { |aP | β(P ) }. Let ô be the mean of ̂X1, ̂X2, . . . , ̂XNs . By Lemma 2 (Hoeffding's inequality), we have that

$$
\Pr \left [ | \hat { \rho } - T r [ O \rho ] | < \epsilon \cdot \Lambda \max _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { | a _ { P } | } { \beta ( P ) } \right ] \geqslant 1 - \delta ,
$$

provided that Ns ⩾ 2 log(2/δ)/ϵ2 which proves the second statement of the lemma.

Moreover, we have

$$
\mathbb { E } [ ( \hat { o } - \text {Tr} [ O \rho ] ) ^ { 2 } ] = \text {Var} [ \hat { o } ] \\
$$

$$
= \frac { 1 } { N _ { s } ^ { 2 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { s } } \text {Var} [ \widehat { X } _ { i } ] \\
$$

$$
\leqslant \frac { 1 } { N _ { s } ^ { 2 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { s } } \mathbb { E } [ \widehat { X } _ { i } ^ { 2 } ]
$$

$$
= \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { a _ { P } ^ { 2 } } { \beta ( P ) } \frac { 1 } { N _ { s } ^ { 2 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { s } } \mathbb { E } [ \hat { x } _ { i } ^ { 2 } ] \\ \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } a _ { P } ^ { 2 } \Lambda ^ { 2 }
$$

$$
& \quad \leqslant \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { a _ { P } ^ { 2 } \Lambda ^ { 2 } } { \beta ( P ) N _ { s } } \ , \\
$$

where the last inequality is due to |x̂i| ⩽ Λ. This proves the first statement of the lemma by setting Ns = Λ2/ϵ2 .

For any given values of the coefficients aP , one can analytically compute the distribution β(P) minimizing the function

$$
\max _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { | a _ { P } | } { \beta ( P ) } \, ,
$$

and run the corresponding measurement protocol, as we demonstrate in the following section. Notice that we assumed β(P) > 0 for aP ̸= 0. This condition will naturally be met with the following settings for β.

## 1. Warm-up: estimating a single observable

The protocol presented in Lemma 5 is parameterized by a distribution β, which defines the probability of measuring each observable P ∈ S in a single round of the protocol. After Ns = Ω(log(1/δ)/ϵ2 ) rounds, each observable P ∈ S is measured β(P)×Ns times, in expectation. Intuitively, more measurement shots should be allocated to the observables P that have larger coefficients aP (in absolute value). The following corollary formalizes this intuition.

Corollary 1. Let O = ∑P∈S aPP be an observable. There exists a randomized measurement protocol which uses 2∥a∥21 log(2/δ)Λ2/ϵ2 measurements on different copies of an unknown state ρ, which outputs an estimate ô satisfying

$$
| \hat { o } - \text {Tr} [ O \rho ] | < \epsilon .
$$

Proof. The corollary follows from Lemma 5 by setting β(·) as

$$
\beta ( P ) = \frac { | a _ { P } | } { \sum _ { P ^ { \prime } \in \mathcal { S } } | a _ { P ^ { \prime } } | } \colon = \frac { | a _ { P } | } { \| a \| _ { 1 } } .
$$

We note that this shot allocation strategy is not novel and has been explored in a number of contexts including the variational quantum algorithms [109, 110]. Crucially, this choice of β minimizes the loss function L(β) = maxP |aP |/β(P), which can be verified using Lagrange multipliers (see Appendix B3). One might wonder whether a simpler protocol, such as allocating an equal probability to all operators in S, could still achieve comparable performance. However, a uniform allocation of measurement shots becomes highly suboptimal when the observable O is concentrated around a few elements of S. In such cases, a uniform strategy would result in an exponentially larger sample complexity, as we demonstrate in the following discussion.

Comparison with uniform allocation. One of the simplest choices for β(·) is the uniform distribution over the set S, that is

$$
\beta ( P ) = \begin{cases} \frac { 1 } { | S | } & \text {if } P \in \mathcal { S } , \\ 0 & \text {otherwise} , \end{cases} \quad ( B 1 6 )
$$

which corresponds to measuring each observable (in S) Ns/|S| times in expectation. By Lemma 5, we have that Ns = 2 log(2/δ)/ϵ2 measurement shots are sufficient to ensure

$$
| \hat { o } _ { \text {uniform} } - T \Upsilon [ O \rho ] | \leqslant \epsilon | \mathcal { S } | \max _ { P } | c _ { P } | < \epsilon \Lambda | \mathcal { S } | \, ,
$$

with probability at least 1 - δ. Setting ϵ = ˜ ϵ Λ|S| , we obtain that Ns = 2|S|2Λ2 log(2/δ)/˜ ϵ2 many measurements are required to achieve an error ˜ ϵ with probability at least 1 - δ. To demonstrate the limitations of this measurement protocol, consider the following extreme example of an observable:

$$
O _ { \text {peaked} } = Z ^ { \otimes n } + \tau \sum _ { P \in \{ I , X , Y , Z \} ^ { \otimes n } \{ Z ^ { \otimes n } \} } \frac { P } { 4 ^ { n } - 1 } .
$$

for some constant τ = Θ(1). In the above expression, the observable is dominated by a single Pauli term Z ⊗n , while all other Pauli strings contribute only marginally with coefficients scaled by an exponentially small factor τ/(4n -1).

On the other hand, we have that

In this case |S| = 4n and Λ = 1 (i.e. S is the set of n qubits Pauli), and thus the samples complexity for the uniform allocation scales as O(16n log(1/δ)/˜ ϵ2 ).

$$
\| a \| _ { 1 } = 1 + \tau \in \Theta ( 1 ) ,
$$

and therefore the sample complexity of the protocol described in Corollary 1 is O(log(1/δ)/˜ ϵ2 ) for achieving error ˜ ϵ and success probability 1 - δ. Crucially, the uniform allocation requires exponentially more measurements.

## 2. Estimating parametrized observables

Throughout this paper, we often encounter parametrized observables O(α) = ∑P∈S cP (α)P, where α is sampled from a given distribution D. Here, we aim at estimating a function ô(α) minimizing the following mean square error:

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left ( T r [ O ( \alpha ) \rho ] - \hat { o } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } .
$$

In such case, designing an optimal measurement protocol is more challenging, since the vector c(α) is now a random variable. Consequently, the measurement distribution β(·) should depend on the distribution D.

Broadly speaking, we show that, while the sample complexity for a single observable (i.e., a fixed value vector α0) is dominated by the 1-norm ∥c(α0)∥1 , the sample complexity for the multiple observables cases can be described via a suitable "effective 1-norm", which we define in the following.

Definition 1 (Effective 1-norms). Let α be a parameter vector, and let O(α) = ∑P∈S cP (α)P be a parametrized observable.

- Let us assume that α is sampled from a distribution D. Then the associated average-case effective 1-norm is defined as

$$
\| c \| _ { 1 , a v g } \coloneqq \sum _ { P \in S } \sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } [ c _ { P } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } ] } } .
$$

- Let us assume that α lies in a set A. Then the associated worst-case effective 1-norm is defined as

$$
\| c \| _ { 1 , w o r s t } \coloneqq \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \max _ { \alpha \in \mathcal { A } } | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | .
$$

Lemma 6 (Mean square error). Let us assume that α is sampled from a distribution D. There exists a randomized measurement protocol which uses Ns = Λ2∥c∥21,avg /ϵ2 measurements on different copies of a state ρ, and outputs an estimate ô(α) for each α satisfying

$$
\sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } , \mathcal { M } } \left ( \text {Tr} [ O ( \alpha ) \rho ] - \hat { o } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } } \leqslant \epsilon \, ,
$$

where EM denotes an average over the measurement outcomes.

Proof. Let β(·) be defined as

We have also seen in Eq. (B12) that

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \mathcal { M } } [ ( \hat { o } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} [ \rho O ( \alpha ) ] ) ^ { 2 } ] \leqslant \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { c _ { P } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } \Lambda ^ { 2 } } { \beta ( P ) N _ { s } } \ ,
$$

which is true for any α. Therefore, averaging over α ∼ D and inserting the definition β(P) leads to the desired result i.e.

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } , \mathcal { M } } [ ( \hat { o } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} [ \rho O ( \alpha ) ] ) ^ { 2 } ] & \leqslant \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } [ c _ { P } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } ] \Lambda ^ { 2 } } { \beta ( P ) N _ { s } } \\ & \| c \| ^ { 2 } \Lambda ^ { 2 }
$$

Finally, we complete the proof by setting Ns = Λ2 ∥c∥21,avg ϵ2

.

Lemma 7 (Maximum error). Let us assume that α lies in the set A. There exists a randomized measurement protocol which uses Ns = 2 log(2/δ)Λ2 ∥c∥21,worst ϵ2 measurements on different copies of a state ρ, and output an estimate ô(α) satisfying

$$
| \text {Tr} [ O ( \alpha ) \rho ] - \hat { o } ( \alpha ) | < \epsilon \, ,
$$

with probability 1- δ (over the internal randomness of the measurement protocol).

Proof. The measurement protocol is similar to Lemma 5 with the sampling distribution β(·) defined as

$$
\beta ( P ) = \frac { \max _ { \alpha \in \mathcal { A } } | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | } { \| c \| _ { 1 , w o r s t } } \ .
$$

Then, for any fixed α ∈ A we can use Hoeffding's inequality (i.e., Lemma 2) as in Lemma 5 to get

$$
| \text {Tr} [ O ( \alpha ) \rho ] - \hat { o } ( \alpha ) | < \tilde { \epsilon } \cdot \Lambda \max _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | } { \beta ( P ) } \, ,
$$

with probability at least 1 - δ using Ns = 2 log(2/δ)/˜ ϵ2 . From previous definition of β(P), we have maxP |cP (α)| β(P) ⩽ ∥c∥1,worst . Thus, substituting ˜ ϵ = ϵ ∥c∥1,worstΛ completes the proof.

Notice that the sampling distribution, β(.) used in Lemma 7 leads to the same bound on the MSE as in Lemma 6, but with the average-case effective 1-norm being replaced by the worst-case one.

$$
& = \frac { \| c \| _ { 1 , a v g } ^ { 2 } \Lambda ^ { 2 } } { N _ { s } } \ .
$$

$$
\beta ( P ) = \frac { \sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } [ c _ { P } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } ] } } { \| c \| _ { 1 , a v g } } .
$$

The measurement protocol here is similar to Lemma 5, but in the fourth step, we use the outcome of the third step, x̂i to define the random variable

$$
\widehat { X } _ { i } ( \alpha ) = \frac { c _ { P _ { i } } ( \alpha ) } { \beta ( P _ { i } ) } \hat { x } _ { i }
$$

for each α. Recall that ̂Xi(α) is an unbiased estimator of Tr[ρO(α)]. Let

$$
\hat { o } ( \alpha ) = \frac { 1 } { N _ { s } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { s } } \widehat { X } _ { i } ( \alpha ) \, .
$$

The solution is given by

## 3. Optimal sampling distributions

In order to find the distribution β(P) that minimize the MSE in Eq. (B26), we used Lagrange multipliers method (see Appendix A5) to minimize the function

$$
\sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } [ c _ { P } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) ] } { \beta ( P ) } \, , \\
$$

under the constraint ∑P∈S β(P) = 1. The appropriate Lagrangian is given by

$$
\mathcal { L } = \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \frac { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } \left [ c _ { P } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) \right ] } { \beta ( P ) } + \lambda \left ( \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \beta ( P ) - 1 \right ) ,
$$

where λ is the Lagrangian multiplier. Next we compute the derivatives with respect to β(P) and equate them to zero. The previous leads to

$$
\frac { \partial \mathcal { L } } { \partial \beta ( P ) } = - \frac { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } [ c _ { P } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) ] } { \beta ( P ) ^ { 2 } } + \lambda = 0 \, .
$$

Then, the solution to the system of equations {∂β(P)L = 0}P∈S with the normalization constraint is

$$
\beta ( P ) = \frac { \sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } [ c _ { P } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) ] } } { \sum _ { P \in S } \sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } [ c _ { P } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) ] } } = \frac { \sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } [ c _ { P } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) ] } } { \| c \| _ { 1 , a g } } \ . \\ \intertext { h o t i m o l $ d i r b u i t i o n $ } \beta ( P ) \, i n v e r _ { P } \, 7 \, i s \, i s i m i o r \, t o \, L \, \text {omme} \, 6 , \, b t h o r h e r o w \, n o d \, t o \, \min i m i z o \\
$$

The derivation of the optimal distribution β(P) in Lemma 7 is similar to Lemma 6, but here we need to minimize Eq. (B31) for any choice of α ∈ A which is equivalent to minimizing the function

$$
\max _ { P } \max _ { \alpha } \frac { | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | } { \beta ( P ) } \, .
$$

Let us define the vector v with entries vP = maxα |cP (α)| β(P) . So, we want to minimize ∥v∥∞ under the constraint ∑P∈S β(P) = 1. Let us consider the k-norm of v and then we will take the limit k →∞. The Lagrangian is given by

$$
\mathcal { L } = \left ( \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } v _ { P } ^ { \ k } \right ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { \kappa } } + \lambda \left ( \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } \beta ( P ) - 1 \right ) \, ,
$$

which is minimized if, for every β(P), we have

$$
- \frac { ( \max _ { \alpha } | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | ) ^ { k } } { \beta ( P ) ^ { k + 1 } } \left ( \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } v _ { P } ^ { k } \right ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { k } } + \lambda = 0 \, .
$$

$$
\beta ( P ) = \frac { ( \max _ { \alpha } | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | ) ^ { \frac { k } { k + 1 } } } { \sum _ { P ^ { \prime } \in \mathcal { S } } ( \max _ { \alpha } | c _ { P ^ { \prime } } ( \alpha ) | ) ^ { \frac { k } { k + 1 } } } \, . \\ k \to \infty \ \, \text {we end up with}
$$

Finally, by taking the limit k →∞, we end up with

$$
\beta ( P ) = \frac { \max _ { \alpha } | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | } { \sum _ { P ^ { \prime } \in S } \max _ { \alpha } | c _ { P ^ { \prime } } ( \alpha ) | } = \frac { \max _ { \alpha } | c _ { P } ( \alpha ) | } { \| c \| _ { 1 , w o r s t } } \ .
$$

## Appendix C: General surrogatability guarantee

## 1. Summary of the key results

In this section we will start by summarising the main results of which our general surrogatability guarantee is comprised. Their proofs are then provided in subsequent sections of this appendix.

with some constant γ ∈ O(1).

Assumption 2. Computing each partial derivative (up to the order κ) requires at most Nd evaluations of the expectation function which scales as

$$
N _ { d } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( m ^ { \kappa } \right ) \, . \\ \\
$$

Assumption 3. The partial derivative can be expressed as a linear combination of expectation functions evaluated at different parameters

$$
\partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N _ { d } } b _ { j } f ( \alpha _ { j } ) \ ,
$$

where each bj is an associated coefficient to f(αj) such that b0 = maxj |bj| with some positive b0 ∈ O(poly(m)), and can be efficiently computed classically. Furthermore, a set of {αj}Nd j=1 depends on α and can be efficiently determined classically. Note that for each combination of the derivatives ∂i1,...,ik the dependence on {bj}Nd j=1 and {αj}Nd j=1 can be different. However, we need the condition b0 =maxj |bj| to hold for all the derivatives.

We note that two well-known approaches to compute gradients that satisfy Assumption 2 and Assumption 3 are parameter shift-rule and numerical finite difference.

In what follows, we present a series of formal statements with the ultimate aim of providing the surrogatability guarantee over the region. We present such result in the form of Supplemental Theorem 3, which is a formal version of Theorem 1 in the main text.

## a. Taylor surrogate construction

We recall that we are interested in expectation functions of the general form

$$
f ( \alpha ) & = \text {Tr} [ \mathcal { E } _ { \alpha } ( \rho ) O ] \, , & & ( C 1 ) \\
$$

where Eα(·) is an arbitrary parameterized quantum channel with m variational parameters α acting on a n-qubit quantum state ρ and O is some Hermitian operator. We are interested in constructing the surrogate f̃κ(α) in the hypercube region Vm(α∗ , r) i.e.,Vm(α∗ , r) := {α} such that αi ∈ [α∗ i - r, α∗ i + r] ∀ i . (C2)

This expectation function can be expressed using a multivariate Taylor's expansion as

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) \, ,
$$

where we introduce two compressed notations for ease of notation

$$
\partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) & = \left ( \frac { \partial ^ { k } f ( \alpha ) } { \partial \alpha _ { i _ { 1 } } \partial \alpha _ { i _ { 2 } } \dots \partial \alpha _ { i _ { k } } } \right ) \Big | _ { \alpha = \alpha ^ { * } } \, , \\ \\ \cdot \quad & ( \alpha _ { i _ { 1 } } , \dots , \alpha _ { i _ { k } } ) = \left ( \frac { \partial ^ { k } f ( \alpha ) } { \partial \alpha _ { i _ { 1 } } \partial \alpha _ { i _ { 2 } } \dots \partial \alpha _ { i _ { k } } } \right ) \Big | _ { \alpha = \alpha ^ { * } } \, ,
$$

$$
\Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) = ( \alpha _ { i _ { 1 } } - \alpha _ { i _ { 1 } } ^ { * } ) ( \alpha _ { i _ { 2 } } - \alpha _ { i _ { 2 } } ^ { * } ) . . ( \alpha _ { i _ { k } } - \alpha _ { i _ { k } } ^ { * } ) \ .
$$

The κth -order surrogate of the expectation function is made by simply truncating the infinite sum in Eq. (C3) to order κ

$$
\widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) \, .
$$

In order to construct the surrogate, we are required to obtain all partial derivatives of the expectation function up to order κ. These partial derivatives can be obtained by evaluating the expectation function f(α) at different points on the landscape.

The following three key assumptions are made for the surrogatability guarantee in our proofs.

Assumption 1. The partial derivatives of f(α) within the region are bounded as

$$
| \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ) | \leqslant \gamma ^ { k } \| O \| _ { \infty } \ , \ \forall \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) \ , \\
$$

with γ ∈ O(1).

## b. Worst-case error

The worst-case error of the κth order surrogate in the region Vm(α∗ , r) around the parameters α∗ is defined as

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { W C } \colon = \max _ { \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) } | \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) |
$$

The following proposition (proved below) tells how the worst-case error scales with respect to the surrogate order.

Proposition 1 (Worst-case error of the surrogate). Consider an expectation function f(α) as defined in Eq. (C1) with ∥O∥∞ ∈ O(1). Furthermore, consider the hypercube region Vm(α∗ , r) around some initial fixed point α∗ with the perturbation r as defined in Eq. (C2) such that

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { m } \right ) \, .
$$

Assume that all the derivatives (up to order k = κ+1) are bounded as

$$
| \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ) | \leqslant \gamma ^ { k } \| O \| _ { \infty } \ , \ \forall \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) \ ,
$$

with γ ∈ O(1) (i.e., Assumption 1).

Then, we have that f̃κ(α) according to Eq. (C6), with the κ order of the truncation, is a classical surrogate of f(α) over this regime with a worst-case error bounded by

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { W C } \leqslant \frac { \| \mathcal { O } \| _ { \infty } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } ( \gamma r m ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } \, .
$$

In particular, an arbitrarily small but constant error ϵWC κ,α∗ ∈ O(1) can be achieved with κ ∈ O(1).

## c. Mean square error

The mean square error (MSE) of the κth order surrogate associated with an uniform distribution Dm(α∗ , r) over the hypercube region Vm(α∗ , r) is defined as

$$
\left ( \epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { M S E } \right ) ^ { 2 } = \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) } \left [ \left ( \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] .
$$

The next proposition (proved below) analytically shows the MSE scaling with respect to the surrogate order.

Proposition 2 (Mean Square Error of the surrogate). Consider an expectation function f(α) as defined in Eq. (C1) with ∥O∥∞ ∈ O(1). Furthermore, consider the hypercube region Vm(α∗ , r) around some initial fixed point α∗ with the perturbation r as defined in Eq. (C2) such that

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { m } } \right ) \, .
$$

Assume that all the derivatives (of order k > κ) are bounded as (i.e., Assumption 1)

$$
| \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ) | \leqslant \gamma ^ { k } \| O \| _ { \infty } \ , \ \forall \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) \ ,
$$

Then, we have that f̃κ(α) according to Eq. (C6), with the κ order of the truncation, is a classical surrogate of f(α) over this regime with the MSE associated with the uniform distribution Dm(α∗ , r) bounded by

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { M S E } \leqslant \left ( \frac { 2 \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } \right ) ^ { \frac { \kappa + 1 } { 2 } } \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } } { \sqrt { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } } e ^ { \frac { \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } } \ .
$$

In particular, for an arbitrarily small but constant error ϵMSE κ,α∗ ∈ O(1) can be achieved for κ ∈ O(1).

## d. Scaling of the number of expectation function evaluations with truncation order

The classical surrogate can be constructed from the partial derivatives at α∗ according to Eq. (C6). These partial derivatives ∂i1,...,ik f(α∗ ) can be computed by evaluating the expectation function f(α) at different parameter values. Let N denote the total number of expectation function evaluations to determine the partial derivatives required to construct the κth order surrogate. The following proposition bounds N in terms of the surrogate order.

Proposition 3 (Scaling of the number of terms). The classical surrogate can be constructed by evaluating the expectation functions at N parameter settings where,

$$
N \leqslant N _ { d } \left [ \frac { e ( m + \kappa - 1 ) } { \kappa } \right ] ^ { \kappa } \, ,
$$

with Nd an upper bound on the number of expectation function evaluations required to compute each partial derivative (up to κ+1 order).

The way we evaluate the expectation function to compute the partial derivatives has not been specified yet. In some certain cases, the expectation function evaluations might be computable classically. However, in general we will need access to quantum computers to estimate expectation function values and in turn the partial derivatives. Since only statistical estimates of the expectation function can be obtained from quantum computers through measurement shots, this leads to an additional error for the surrogate due to the statistical fluctuation.

## e. General surrogation guarantee

The following Supplemental Theorem presents the final surrogatability guarantee taking into account the presence of shot noise. Here we assume that the expectation function can be estimated on a quantum computer by measuring in the eigenbasis of the observable O. That is, given an eigendecomposition O = ∑j λj|λj⟩⟨λj| where λj is an eigenvalue with an associated eigenstate |λj⟩, an unbiased estimator of the expectation function after Ns measurement shots can be expressed as

$$
\widehat { f } ( \alpha ) = \frac { 1 } { N _ { s } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N _ { s } } \hat { o } _ { i } \, ,
$$

where ôi is an outcome of ith measurement which takes a value λj with a probability pj = Tr[Eα(ρ)|λj⟩⟨λj|]. For observables of complex many-body systems it may not be straightforward to measure in the eigenbasis of O. In this case, it is instead natural to expand O as a sum of 'easier' operators to measure (e.g., a sum of Paulis). While we do not explicitly present this case here, the generalization is straightforward.

Supplemental Theorem 3 (General Surrogatability Guarantee, Formal). Consider an expectation function f(α) as defined in Eq. (C1) with ∥O∥∞ ∈ O(1). Assume that the derivatives are bounded as

$$
| \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ) | \leqslant & \| O \| _ { \infty } \gamma ^ { k } \ \ , \ \forall \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) \ ,
$$

with γ ∈ O(1) (i.e., Assumption 1). We additionally assume that each partial derivative (up to the order κ) can be written as a linear combination of at most Nd expectation function evaluations such that

$$
N _ { d } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( m ^ { \kappa } \right )
$$

(i.e., Assumption 2) and where the biggest coefficient in absolute value scales at most as b0 ∈ O(poly(m)) (i.e., Assumption 3). Moreover, we assume that each of these expectation function evaluations can be estimated by measuring in the eigenbasis on a quantum computer as in Eq. (C19).

- Worst-case error: when the perturbation r scales as

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { m } \right ) \, ,
$$

with a number of measurement shots Ns and probability at least 1-δ (for any δ ∈ Θ(1)) we can construct ̂f̃κ(α) such that,

$$
\left | \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | & \leqslant \| O \| _ { \infty } \left ( b _ { 0 } N _ { d } e ^ { r m } \sqrt { \frac { 2 \log ( 2 / \delta ) } { N _ { s } } } + \frac { ( \gamma r m ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \right ) \, .
$$

Thus the worst-case error can be reduced to an arbitrarily small constant, ∣∣∣∣̂f̃κ(α) - f(α)∣∣∣∣ ∈ Θ(1) by increasing the order κ with κ ∈ O(1) and the number of shots with Ns ∈ O(poly(m)).

- Average case error: when the perturbation r scales as

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { m } } \right ) \, ,
$$

with Ns measurement shots, we can construct ̂f̃κ(α) such that,

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , M } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] & \leqslant 2 \left ( \frac { b _ { 0 } N _ { d } ( \kappa + 1 ) ( r m ) ^ { \kappa } \| O \| _ { \infty } } { \sqrt { N _ { s } } \kappa ! } \right ) ^ { 2 } + 2 \left ( \frac { 2 \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } \right ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } e ^ { \frac { 2 \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } } \, ,
$$

Thus the mean square error can be arbitrarily reduced to any constant in Θ(1) by increasing the order κ with κ ∈ O(1) and the number of shots Ns with Ns ∈ O(poly(m)). Then by applying Markov's inequality 1 we find the informal version of the theorem.

Supplemental Theorem 3 is the formal version of Theorem 1 from the main text. Indeed, we can set both the truncation and the empirical mean square errors to be (ϵ/2)2 . We have seen that with r ∈ O ( 1 √m ), the mean square truncation error can be made arbitrarily small by increasing κ ∈ O(1). Then, equating both errors from the expression in Eq. (C25) leads to

$$
N _ { s } \in \mathcal { O } ( m ^ { \kappa } b _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } N _ { d } ^ { 2 } ) = \mathcal { O } ( \text {poly} ( m ) ) \, ,
$$

where we used Assumptions 2 and 3 for the scaling of both b0 and Nd. Therefore, using polynomial resources, we can get an arbitrarily small mean square error. A similar analysis can be made the worst-case error guarantee with r ∈ O(1/m) to get

$$
N _ { s } \in \mathcal { O } ( N _ { d } ^ { 2 } b _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } \log ( 1 / \delta ) ) = \mathcal { O } ( \text {poly} ( m ) ) \, .
$$

We note that in the proof of Supplemental Theorem 3, the surrogate is expressed as the linear combination of the expectation function evaluated at different parameters. In order to construct a statistical estimate of the surrogate, we individually estimate each term in the sum and also the total measurement shout budget is evenly distributed to all the terms. This strategy is sub-optimal in general since it could be the case that there are terms in the sum that commute with each other and hence they can be simultaneously estimated and/or the total shot budget could be distributed in a more informative manner. Nevertheless, we now show that even with this sub-optimal strategy one can achieve a surrogate that achieves an arbitrarily small constant error with high probability with a polynomial number of measurement shots.

Note: In principle, the analysis can be generalized to allow for independent perturbation radii for each parameter. The intuition is simple: in the derivation of the Taylor remainder in Eq. (C63), the dominant contribution arises from the variance of the sum of the parameter variations, suggesting that the expression mr2 could be replaced by ∑mi=1 r2 i where δi ∈ [-ri, ri]. Nevertheless, establishing this generalization with full rigor would require considerably more technical work and would add little conceptual value to the main results. For this reason, and to keep the presentation focused, we restrict the analysis to hypercubes, while noting that the extension follows the same intuition.

## f. Guarantee that our assumptions are met by common parameterized quantum circuits

The following proposition establishes that a broad family of parameterized quantum circuits (indeed, essentially all currently considered by the community currently with independent parameters between circuit layers) satisfy the assumptions of our bounds.

Proposition 4 (Bounded derivatives for expectation value with parametrized unitary channel). Let us assume a expectation function of the form of Eq. (1) i.e. f(α) = Tr[ρU † (α)OU(α)], where U(α) = ∏ml=1 VlUl(αl) such that Vl are arbitrary non-parametrized unitaries and each Ul(αl) = exp(-iHlαl) is a parametrised gate with generator Hl that is hermitian. Then, the k-th order derivatives of the form ∂ k f(α) ∏ml=1 ∂αkl l := ∂ k f(α) where ∑ml=1 kl = k and kl ∈ N for each l are bounded by

$$
| \partial ^ { k } f ( \alpha ) | \leqslant & \| O \| _ { \infty } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { l } } \\
$$

$$
\leqslant \| O \| _ { \infty } \left ( 2 \max _ { l } \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } \right ) ^ { k }
$$

Corollary 2 (Surrogation Guarantee for Unitary Channel). Consider a generic expectation function f(α) as defined in Eq. (1) with m independent parameters such that the spectral norm of each generators as well as the initial observable one are O(1), and assume that (up to) order-κ partial derivatives can be estimated efficiently with quantum computers. It is possible to efficiently classically surrogate f(α) over any uniformly sampled hypercube Dm(α∗ , r) around an arbitrary point α∗ , with

$$
r \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { m } } \right ) \, .
$$

In our general surrogate guarantee, Assumptions 2 and 3 can be argued from a practical perspective. Let us clarify the scope of this theorem by first considering the following simple example. Assume a circuit composed by uncorrelated Pauli rotations and any non-parametrized gates (not restricted to near-Clifford here). In this case, the parameter-shift rule, which satisfies both Assumptions 2 and 3, can be readily applied. Indeed, any order of derivatives with respect to a single parameters involves two loss evaluations with coefficients magnitude 1/2. From this, we can directly state that b0 ∈ O(1). For k-th order partial derivatives, the largest number of loss evaluations correspond to derivatives with respect to k distinct parameters which leads to up-to 2k loss evaluations in total (with coefficients magnitude 1/2k ) [111]. This means that truncating at order κ involves at most Nd ∈ O(2κ ) loss evaluation for each partial derivatives considered in the surrogate. Therefore, both assumptions are satisfied. For this case, we provide a numerical study in Appendix D where we use Taylor surrogate iteratively to approximate a ground state.

The standard parameter-shift rule applies to gates whose generators have a two distinct eigenvalues, including single-qubit rotations and multi-qubit Pauli rotations. More generally, we can use the generalized parameter shift rule [90] to compute derivatives of generators with more than two disctinct eigenvalues. One example are Givens rotations whose generators are sums of commuting Pauli operators. These require four circuit evaluations.

## g. How the polynomial guarantees break down for larger patch sizes

For completeness, we note that if we want the resource requirements to scale at most polynomially with the system size and number of variational parameters, the only region for which we 2 could prove generic guarantees for is the aforementioned r ∈ O(1/√m). Indeed, this ensures that the terms we require to compute and the MSE ϵ remain constant.

We can see this as follows. We start by recalling that the number of terms we need to compute, given by Proposition 3, scales as

$$
N \in \mathcal { O } ( m ^ { w \kappa } ) \, , \\ \subset [ 1 , 0 ] \, \colon = \, \subset
$$

where κ is the truncation order and w ∈ [1, 2] is some constant, and the MSE given in Proposition 2 is proportional to

$$
\epsilon ^ { 2 } \leqslant \frac { \left ( c m r ^ { 2 } \right ) } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } ^ { \kappa + 1 } e ^ { c m r ^ { 2 } } \, ,
$$

where c = 2γ 2/3 ∈ O(1) is a constant. Next we will proceed to show what happens when we want to extend beyond the r ∈ O(1/√m).

2 We do not rule out that others using a different proof strategy could not prove guarantees in a larger region [111], although we think this unlikely.

Let us first simplify the MSE expression using Stirling approximation for the factorial:

$$
k ! \geq \sqrt { 2 \pi k } \left ( \frac { k } { e } \right ) ^ { k } \geq \left ( \frac { k } { e } \right ) ^ { k } \, .
$$

Therefore, the MSE ϵ can be bounded as follows

$$
\epsilon ^ { 2 } \leqslant \left ( \frac { c e m r ^ { 2 } } { \kappa + 1 } \right ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } e ^ { c m r ^ { 2 } } \, .
$$

From the previous equation, we see that in order to make the error bound arbitrarily small, we would necessarily need cemr2 < κ + 1. Let us fix κ + 1 = Kcemr2 ∈ O(mr2 ) where K > 1 is a constant. Then we can rewrite the upper bound in the MSE from the previous equation as

$$
\epsilon ^ { 2 } \leqslant \left ( \frac { 1 } { K } \right ) ^ { K e c m r ^ { 2 } } e ^ { c m r ^ { 2 } } = \left ( \frac { e } { K ^ { K e } } \right ) ^ { c m r ^ { 2 } } \, .
$$

Since we are interested in extending r to larger region (beyond O(1/√m)), we can assume r ∈ O(1/mβ ) with 0 ⩽ β < 1/2. In this case, we would have κ ∈ O(mr2 ) = O(m1-2β ) where 1 - 2β > 0. Substituting back the relation between κ and m into the number of resources required in Eq. (C31), the count scales as

$$
N \in \mathcal { O } \left ( m ^ { w m ^ { 1 \ \ 2 \beta } } \right ) \ ,
$$

which scales super-exponentially for β = 0, where r ∈ O(1).

More positively, we can reduce to sub-exponential (albeit still super-polynomial) if 0 < β < 1/2. Note that if r decreases logarithmically with m, the resources also scales super-polynomially.

Pauli Propagation. The previous observations can be equivalently shown for Pauli propagation truncation case (see Appendix E). In this case, the maximal resource requirement (see Eq. (E56)) is proportional to

$$
N \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \, ,
$$

whereas the error will be (see Supplemental Theorem 4) of the following form

$$
\epsilon ^ { 2 } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m r ^ { 2 } } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \, .
$$

To make the error arbitrarily small, we would need at least κ ∈ Ω(mr2 ). So assuming r ∈ O(1/mβ ) with 0 ⩽ β < 1/2, we have κ ∈ O(m1-2β ). Since the resource scales exponentially with κ ∈ O(m1-2β ), it will be exponential with m for the case β = 0, i.e., constant r and sub-exponential, but super-polynomial for 0 < β < 1/2. Similarly, if κ decreases logarithmically with m, the resource scaling will be super-polynomial (but sub-exponential in general). Obviously, the case r ∼ 1 makes no sense here since sine can be larger than cosine. Also, setting κ =m, means that we are not truncating anymore.

Within the Pauli-propagation framework, the required number of terms is inherently problem-dependent. While the worst-case complexity is super-polynomial, Pauli propagation can still be practical in specific settings. Eq. (C37) should thus be interpreted as an upper bound arising from a maximal-splitting scenario.

## 2. Absolute error: Proof of Proposition 1

Proof. The worst-case error of the surrogate f̃κ(α) of the order κ is written as

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { W C } \colon = \max _ { \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) } | \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) | \, ,
$$

where fα is the expectation function, and Vm(α∗ , r) is the hypercube in the parameter space such that

$$
\alpha _ { i } \in [ \alpha _ { i } ^ { * } - r , \alpha _ { i } ^ { * } + r ] \, .
$$

By invoking the Taylor Reminder Theorem (i.e., Supplemental Theorem 2), we see that this is equivalent to bounding the reminder of the Taylor expansion. In particular, by denoting the parameters corresponding to the worst case error

$$
\alpha ^ { \prime } = \underset { \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) } { \arg \max } | \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) | \, ,
$$

we can use Supplemental Theorem 2 to explicitly write the worst-case error as

$$
\varepsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { \text {WC} } & = | \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ^ { \prime } ) - f ( \alpha ^ { \prime } ) | \\ & = \left | \frac { 1 } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } ( \alpha ^ { \prime } , \alpha ^ { * } ) \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } f ( \vartheta ) \right | \, ,
$$

where ϑ = τα′ + (1 - τ)α∗ for some τ ∈ [0, 1].

By applying the triangle inequality we can further simplify this expression to obtain

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { \ W C } = \left | \frac { 1 } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } ( \alpha ^ { \prime } , \alpha ^ { * } ) \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } f ( \vartheta ) \right |
$$

$$
& \leqslant \frac { 1 } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \left | \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } ( \alpha ^ { \prime } , \alpha ^ { * } ) \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } f ( \vartheta ) \right | \\
$$

$$
= \frac { 1 } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \left | \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } ( \alpha ^ { \prime } , \alpha ^ { * } ) \right | \left | \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } f ( \vartheta ) \right |
$$

where in the last equality we use that the absolute value of the product is the product of absolute values, i.e., |xy| = |x||y|.

Next, let us focus on the terms ∂i1,...iκ+1 f(ϑ). Since the assumption in Eq (C12) of Proposition 1 holds, the derivatives in the previous sum are bounded as

$$
| \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ) | \leqslant & \| O \| _ { \infty } \gamma ^ { k } \ \ , \ \forall \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) \ .
$$

Combining the previous result with Eq. (C46), we can simplify the bound as

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { W C } \leqslant \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } \gamma ^ { \kappa + 1 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } | \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) | \, .
$$

Next we can recall the definition of ∆i1,...,iκ+1=1(α′ ,α∗ ) in Eq. (C5)

$$
\Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k + 1 } } ( \alpha ^ { \prime } , \alpha ^ { * } ) & = ( \alpha ^ { \prime } _ { i _ { 1 } } - \alpha ^ { * } _ { i _ { 1 } } ) ( \alpha ^ { \prime } _ { i _ { 2 } } - \alpha ^ { * } _ { i _ { 2 } } ) . . ( \alpha ^ { \prime } _ { i _ { k + 1 } } - \alpha ^ { * } _ { i _ { k + 1 } } ) \\ & = \delta ^ { \prime } _ { i _ { 1 } } \delta ^ { \prime } _ { i _ { 2 } } . . \delta ^ { \prime } _ { i _ { k + 1 } } \ ,
$$

where we denote δ′k = α′k -α∗k . Therefore, we have

$$
\epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { W C } \leqslant \frac { \| O | _ { \infty } \gamma ^ { \kappa + 1 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \left | \delta _ { i _ { 1 } } ^ { \prime } \dots \delta _ { i _ { \kappa + 1 } } ^ { \prime } \right |
$$

$$
\leqslant \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } \gamma ^ { \kappa + 1 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { \kappa + 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } r ^ { \kappa + 1 } \\
$$

$$
= \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } ( \gamma r m ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } \, ,
$$

where in the second inequality we used that maxαi |αi - α∗ i | = r for all the αi and to reach the last line we use ∑mi=1 1 = m for all indices. Notice that r ∈ O(1/m), γ ∈ O(1) and ∥O∥∞ ∈ O(1) implies γrm ∈ O(1) and therefore, the error is O(1) and decreases with κ, which completes the proof of the proposition.

Proof. The MSE is of the form

$$
( \epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { M S E } ) ^ { 2 } = \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r _ { m } ) } \left [ \left ( \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ]
$$

$$
= \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r _ { m } ) } \left [ \left ( \sum _ { k = \kappa + 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ]
$$

where Dm(α∗ , rm) is a uniform distribution of α in the hypercube region, i.e., Vm(α∗ , rm) = {α} such that αi ∈ [α∗ i - rm, α∗ i +rm] for all i. Note that we explicitly add the subscript m in rm indicating that it is associated with α with m parameters. This will come in handy later in our proof. To reach the second equality, we use the definition of the Taylor expansion on the expectation function in Eq. (C3) and the definition of the surrogate in Eq. (C6) to see that f̃κ(α) - f(α) is an infinite sum of terms.

To further proceed, we introduce a perturbation vector δ := α-α∗ , whose i-th components is δi = αi -α∗ i for any index i. Then, we also denote the average of δ over the perturbed region Eδ[·] := Eδ∼Dm(0,rm)[·] where Dm(0, rm) is a uniform distribution over the region Vm(0, rm) = {δ} with δi ∈ [-rm, rm]. More specifically, Dm(0, rm) is obtained by sampled independently each component δi over [-rm, rm]. Then, the MSE can be written as

$$
\left ( \epsilon _ { \kappa , \alpha ^ { * } } ^ { M S E } \right ) ^ { 2 } = \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) } \left ( \sum _ { k = \kappa + 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \\
$$

$$
= \sum _ { k , l = \kappa + 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { 1 } { l ! k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \sum _ { j _ { 1 } , \dots , j _ { l } = 1 } ^ { m } \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } _ { m } ( \alpha ^ { * } , r ) } \left [ \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) \Delta _ { j _ { 1 } , \dots , j _ { l } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) \right ] \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \partial _ { j _ { 1 } , \dots , j _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \\ \\ \infty \quad m \quad m \\
$$

$$
\leqslant \sum _ { k , l = \kappa + 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { 1 } { l ! k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \sum _ { j _ { 1 } , \dots , j _ { l } = 1 } ^ { m } \mathbb { E } _ { \delta } \left [ \delta _ { i _ { 1 } } \dots \delta _ { i _ { k } } \delta _ { j _ { 1 } } \dots \delta _ { j _ { l } } \right ] \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } \gamma ^ { k + l }
$$

= \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } \sum _ { k , l = \kappa + 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { \gamma ^ { k + l } } { l ! k ! } \mathbb { E } _ { \delta } \left [ \sum _ { i _ { 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \delta _ { i _ { 1 } } \cdots \sum _ { i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \delta _ { i _ { k } } \sum _ { j _ { 1 } = 1 } ^ { m } \delta _ { j _ { 1 } } \cdots \sum _ { j _ { l } = 1 } ^ { m } \delta _ { j _ { l } } \right ]

where the inequality is by the assumption of the bounded derivatives as specified in Eq. (C16),

together with the fact that the averages of the perturbations are always non-negative due to the symmetry in the perturbations. The central limit theorem can now be applied to Xm (see Supplemental Theorem 1), which essentially states that when m approaches infinity, the probability distribution of the random variable Xm approaches a normal distribution with the zero mean and variance equal to one, i.e., N(0, 1). As stated in Appendix A3, the moments of a random variable under N(0, 1) are

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \delta } [ X _ { m \to \infty } ^ { a } ] = \begin{cases} 0 & \text {if } a \text { is odd} \\ ( a - 1 ) ! ! & \text {if } a \text { is even} \end{cases} \quad ( C 6 6 )

where in the second line we use the binomial expansion (x+y)a = ∑ai=0 (ai )xi ya-i and the linearity of the expectation, while in the inequality we dropped all the terms (which are all non-negative) except for i = a.
