= \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } \left ( \frac { 2 m \gamma ^ { 2 } r _ { m } ^ { 2 } } { 3 } \right ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } e ^ { \frac { 2 m \gamma ^ { 2 } r _ { m } ^ { 2 } } { 3 } } \, ,
$$

where to reach the last equality we use Eq. (C63). We remind again that we explicitly use the condition that rm ∈ O ( 1 √m ) in Eq. (C72) for the bound to be valid. Moreover, assuming γ ∈ O(1) implies mγ 2 r2m ∈ O(1). Therefore, we can see that the MSE above can be made arbitrarily small by increasing κ. Especially, if ∥O∥∞ ∈ O(1) we have ϵMSE κ,α∗ ∈ O(1).

## 4. Scaling of the number of expectation function terms for given surrogation order: Proof of Proposition 3

Proof. We start this proof by recalling the expression of the surrogate in Eq. (C6)

$$
\widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ^ { m } \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } ) \, .
$$

Now, let us rewrite this expression as a sum over unique partial derivatives. Indeed, we can group terms using the symmetry of the partial derivatives (e.g. ∂i,jf = ∂j,if) to give

$$
\widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \partial _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \Delta _ { i _ { 1 } , \dots , i _ { k } } ( \alpha , \alpha ^ { * } )
$$

$$
= \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { i _ { 1 } , i _ { 2 } , \dots , i _ { k } = 1 } ^ { m } \prod _ { i _ { l } = i _ { 1 } } ^ { i _ { k } } \left ( \delta _ { i _ { l } } \partial _ { i _ { l } } \right ) f ( \alpha ^ { * } )
$$

where we noted ∆i1,..,ik (α,α∗ ) = ∏ik is=i1 δis , with δil = αil - α∗ il . We can recognize that this expression contains all the terms of a multinomial expansion. Indeed, the sum of products ∑mi1,i2,..,ik=1 ∏ik il=i1 (δil ∂il ) contains all the possible combinations of products (δil ∂il ) up to a maximum power of k. Therefore, we can just rewrite this expression in a more compact form making use of the multinomial expression. That is, as

$$
\widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { \left ( \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \delta _ { i } \frac { \partial } { \partial \alpha _ { i } } \right ) ^ { k } } { k ! } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \\
$$

$$
= \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { 1 } { k ! } \sum _ { k _ { 1 } + \dots + k _ { m } = k } \left ( \sum _ { k _ { 1 } , \dots , k _ { m } } \right ) \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \left ( \delta _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } \partial _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } \right ) f ( \alpha ^ { * } )
$$

$$
= \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \sum _ { k _ { 1 } + \dots + k _ { m } = k } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } \partial _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } \right ) f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \, ,
$$

where the second equality is obtained by expanding the multinomial. In this expression we have introduced kl ∈ N which is the order of the derivative with respect to parameter αl for each l ∈ {1, 2, ...,m}. The last equality is obtained by simplifying the expression of the multinomial coefficient and the factorial term:

$$
\begin{pmatrix} k \\ k _ { 1 } , \dots , k _ { m } \end{pmatrix} \frac { 1 } { k ! } = \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { 1 } { k _ { l } ! } \, .
$$

The number of different terms N that need to be evaluated in order to construct the surrogate/simulate the expectation function landscape corresponds to the number of different derivatives. Indeed, for example in the case of surrogation, each derivative would be a different term that would need to be estimated using a quantum computer. In other words, we assume that each partial derivative can be obtained by at worst measuring Nd different parameter settings of the expectation function on a quantum computer. For the kth order in the surrogate, there exists (m+k-1 k ) unique partial derivatives (which is the number of possible k = (k1, k2, ..., km) ∈ Nm such that ∑ml=1 kl = k), leading to at most (m+k-1 k )Nd expectation values to be measured in a quantum computer. Then we can readily compute an upper-bound in the total number of terms N we will have to measure by summing all the orders in the surrogate.

$$
N = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \left ( ^ { m + k - 1 } _ { k } \right ) N _ { d } \leqslant N _ { d } \left [ \frac { e ( m + \kappa - 1 ) } { \kappa } \right ] ^ { \kappa } \, ,
$$

where the inequality is obtained using (nk) ⩽ nk k! as follows

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \binom { m + k - 1 } { k } & \leqslant \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { ( m + k - 1 ) ^ { k } } { k ! } \\
$$

$$
= \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { \kappa ^ { k } } { k ! } \left ( \frac { m + k - 1 } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { k } \\
$$

$$
& \leqslant \left ( \frac { m + \kappa - 1 } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { \kappa ^ { k } } { k ! } \\ & \leqslant ( \frac { \kappa } { \kappa } ) ^ { 2 } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { \kappa ^ { k } } { k ! }
$$

$$
\leqslant \left ( \frac { e ( m + \kappa - 1 ) } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } ,
$$

where the second inequality is obtained by noticing that the term ( m+k-1 κ )k reaches its largest value when k = κ (as it increases with k for all κ ⩽ m- 1), and the last inequality is obtained by adding terms in the sum up to k →∞ and recognising the exponential series i.e. ∑∞k=0 κk k! = eκ .

## 5. General surrogation guarantee: Proof of Supplemental Theorem 3

Proof. Assumption 2 and Assumption 3 implies that the κ-order surrogate f̃κ(α) can be expressed as a linear combination of expectation functions evaluated at different parameters. In particular, from Assumption 3, we can express each partial derivatives in Eq. (C95) as

$$
\prod _ { l } \frac { ^ { k } } { \partial \alpha _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N _ { d } } b _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } f ( \alpha _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } ) \, ,
$$

where k = (k1, k2, ..., km) ∈ Nm. Therefore, Eq. (C95) can be rewritten as follows

$$
\widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \sum _ { k _ { 1 } + \dots + k _ { m } = k } \prod _ { l } \frac { \delta _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N _ { d } } b _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } f ( \alpha _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } )
$$

$$
= \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \sum _ { | k | = k } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N _ { d } } \left ( b _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { \delta _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } \right ) f ( \alpha _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } ) \\ = \sum _ { N _ { d } } ^ { N _ { d } } \left ( \sum _ { k _ { l } = 1 } ^ { m } \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { N _ { d } } \left ( b _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { \delta _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } \right ) f ( \alpha _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } )
$$

$$
= \sum _ { | k | \leqslant \kappa } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N _ { d } } \left ( b _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { \delta _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } \right ) f ( \alpha _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } )
$$

$$
= \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { N } c _ { l } ( \alpha ) f ( \alpha _ { l } ) ,
$$

where in the last equality we use an index l for each possible choice of (k, j) such that k ∈ Nm with |k| = k1+...+km ⩽ κ and j ∈ {1, ...,Nd} (notice that there are N possible choice of (k, j)). So, we defined f(αl) = f(α(k) j ) and cl(α) = b(k) j ∏mj=1 δkj j kj! . The total number of terms in the sum, N is given in Eq. (C97) as well as its upper bound (see Proposition 3).

(i). The worst-case error with the perturbation r ∈ O(1/m).

The total error can be upper bounded by the sum of both truncation error and shot noise error using the triangle inequality:

$$
\left | \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | \leq & \left | \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right | + \left | \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | \, .
$$

The term | f̃κ(α)-f(α)| can be bounded using Proposition 1. We now focus on the term ∣∣∣∣̂f̃κ(α) - f̃κ(α)∣∣∣∣. Lemma 7 can be applied to the surrogate of the expectation function expressed in the form of Eq. (C106) by noticing that for each l we can define operator O(αl) such that f(αl) = Tr[ρO(αl)] and ∥O(αl)∥∞ ⩽ ∥O∥∞ with associate coefficients cl(α). Let cα be the vector of length N with the lth component cl(α). Therefore, the measurement strategy presented in Lemma 7 can be applied to output an estimator ̂f̃κ(α) of f̃κ(α) such that Ns measurements on different copies of ρ are sufficient to ensure

$$
\left | \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right | < \| c _ { \alpha } \| _ { 1 , w o r s t } \| O \| _ { \infty } \sqrt { \frac { 2 \log ( 2 / \delta ) } { N _ { s } } } \ ,
$$

with probability at least 1-δ. Now, using the assumption that the coefficients b(k) j are bounded by b0, i.e. |b(k) j | ⩽ b0, we have

$$
\| c _ { \alpha } \| _ { 1 } = \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { N } | c _ { l } ( \alpha ) |
$$

$$
= \sum _ { | k | \leqslant \kappa } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N _ { d } } | b _ { j } ^ { ( k ) } | \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { | \delta _ { l } | ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } & & ( C 1 1 0 ) \\ & & ^ { m } | \delta _ { j } | k _ { l }
$$

$$
& | k | \ll n ^ { 5 } \quad ^ { m } \prod _ { k _ { l } = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { | \delta _ { l } | ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } \\ & \leqslant b _ { 0 } N _ { d } \sum _ { | k | \leqslant \kappa } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { | \delta _ { l } | ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } \\
$$

$$
\leqslant b _ { 0 } N _ { d } \sum _ { | k | \leqslant \kappa } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { r ^ { k _ { l } } } { k _ { l } ! } & & ( C 1 1 2 ) \\
$$

$$
= b _ { 0 } N _ { d } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { ( r m ) ^ { k } } { k ! } \ ,
$$

where the second inequality follows from |δl| ⩽ r and the last equality is obtained by recognising the multinomial sum ∑|k|=k ( k k1,...,km)∏ml=1 rkl = (mr)k where ( k k1,...,km) = k! ∏ml=1 kl! . Notice that this bound is valid for any α ∈ Vm(α∗ , r) due to the second inequality, so this is also an upper bound of ∥cα∥1,worst . Additionally, we can upper bound Eq. (C113) by

$$
b _ { 0 } N _ { d } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { ( r m ) ^ { k } } { k ! } \leqslant b _ { 0 } N _ { d } e ^ { r m } \ ,
$$

which is obtained by extending the limit of the sum to ∞ and recognising the exponential series. Assuming that rm ∈ O(1) ensures that erm ∼ O(1). By inserting previous bound on the effective 1-norm in Eq. (C108) to bound the shot noise error, and using Proposition 1 to bound the truncation error, the total error is bounded as follows

$$
\left | \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | & \leqslant b _ { 0 } N _ { d } e ^ { r m } \| O \| _ { \infty } \sqrt { \frac { 2 \log ( 2 / \delta ) } { N _ { s } } } + \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } ( \gamma r m ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } \, ,
$$

which happens with probability at least 1 - δ. Assuming that b0,Nd ∈ O(poly(m)), then we can see that using polynomial number of shots is enough to decreases the empirical error while the truncation error can be reduced by increasing κ.

## (ii). The average-case error with the perturbation r ∈ O(1/√m).

First, let us bound the total mean square error by both contribution of the empirical error and the truncation error using that (x+ y)2 ⩽ 2(x2 + y2 ) for any reals x and y. This leads to

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , \mathcal { M } } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant 2 \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , \mathcal { M } } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] + 2 \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } \left [ \left ( \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] .
$$

Notice that the average here is over both the random parameters α and the output of the measurement denoted by M (except for the truncation error which depends only on α).

Now, the estimate ̂f̃κ(α) obtained by following the measurement protocol described in Lemma 6 leads to the following upper bound on the empirical mean square error

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , \mathcal { M } } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } \| o \| _ { 1 , a v g } ^ { 2 } } { N _ { s } } \, ,
$$

where ∥c∥1,avg is the averaged effective 1-norm (see Definition 1) of the vector cα.

Finally, combining this result together with the MSE upper bound of the surrogate from Proposition 2 leads to

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , \mathcal { M } } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \tilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant 2 \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } \| c \| _ { 1 , a v g } ^ { 2 } } { N _ { s } } + 2 \left ( \frac { 2 \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } \right ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } e ^ { \frac { 2 \cdot \frac { \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } } { 3 } } \, .
$$

Notice that Eq. (C113) is also a valid upper bound for ∥c∥1,avg as it correspond to a worst case 1-norm obtained by upper bounding each |cl(α)|. Therefore, the sum in Eq. (C113) is bounded as follows

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \frac { ( r m ) ^ { k } } { k ! } \leqslant ( \kappa + 1 ) \frac { ( r m ) ^ { \kappa } } { \kappa ! } \ ,
$$

under the condition that rm > κ from Eq. (A8) (since r ∈ O(1/√m) , we have that rm ∈ O(√m)). From the assumptions, the bound on the effective norm scales polynomially with the number of parameters. Finally, we have

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , \mathcal { M } } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] & \leqslant 2 \left ( \frac { b _ { 0 } N _ { d } ( \kappa + 1 ) ( r m ) ^ { \kappa } \| O \| _ { \infty } } { \sqrt { N _ { s } } \kappa ! } \right ) ^ { 2 } + 2 \left ( \frac { 2 \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } \right ) ^ { \kappa + 1 } \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } } { ( \kappa + 1 ) ! } e ^ { \frac { 2 \gamma ^ { 2 } m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } } \, .
$$

Notice that both measurement strategies from Lemma 6 and Lemma 7 can be used to get previous bound as we upper-bounded the average-case 1-norm by the worst-case one.

## 6. Parametrised quantum circuits that satisfy assumptions: Proof of Proposition 4 and Corollary 2

Corollary 2 is a direct consequence of Supplemental Theorem 3 and Proposition 4. Let us first derive Proposition 4.

Proof. The expectation function considered here is of the form of Eq. (C1) with unitary channel, i.e. f(α) = Tr[ρU † (α)OU(α)], where U(α) = ∏ml=1 VlUl(αl) such that Vl are arbitrary non-parametrized unitaries and each Ul(αl) = exp(-iHlαl) is a parametrised gate with Hermitian generator Hl. Now, let us bound the k-th order derivatives of the form ∂ k f(α) ∏ml=1 ∂αkl l := ∂ k f(α) where ∑ml=1 kl = k and kl ∈ N for each l. First, let us use Hölder inequality as follows

$$
| \partial ^ { k } f ( \alpha ) | & = | \text {Tr} [ \partial ^ { k } U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) ] | \\ & < \| \partial ^ { k } U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) \|
$$

$$
= \left \| \partial ^ { k } ( U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) ) \right \| _ { \infty } ,
$$

$$
& \leqslant \| \rho \| _ { 1 } \| \partial ^ { k } U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) \| _ { \infty } & & ( C 1 2 2 ) \\ & = \| \partial ^ { k } ( U \dagger ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) ) \| _ { \infty } & & ( C 1 2 3 )
$$

where the last equality is given by the normalisation of quantum states. Now, the goal is to show that

$$
\| \partial ^ { k } ( U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) ) \| _ { \infty } \leqslant \| O \| _ { \infty } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { l } } \ ,
$$

by induction on l. First notice that the operator we want to bound can be rewritten as follows

$$
\partial ^ { k } \left ( ( U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) ) = \frac { \partial ^ { k _ { 1 } } } { \partial \alpha _ { 1 } ^ { k _ { 1 } } } \left ( U _ { 1 } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { 1 } ) V _ { 1 } ^ { \dagger } \left ( \dots \frac { \partial ^ { k _ { m } } } { \partial \alpha _ { m } ^ { k _ { m } } } \left ( U _ { m } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { m } ) V _ { m } ^ { \dagger } O V _ { m } U _ { m } ( \alpha _ { m } ) \right ) \dots \right ) V _ { 1 } U _ { 1 } ( \alpha _ { 1 } ) \right ) .
$$

Then, each derivative consists in applying a commutator with the generator or more formally for any operator Al+1 (which will be specify later), we have

$$
\frac { \partial ^ { k _ { l } } } { \partial \alpha _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } \left ( U _ { l } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { l } ) A _ { l + 1 } U _ { l } ( \alpha _ { l } ) \right ) & = \frac { \partial ^ { k _ { l } - 1 } } { \partial \alpha _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } - 1 } } \left ( U _ { l } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { l } ) i [ H _ { l } , A _ { l + 1 } ] U _ { l } ( \alpha _ { l } ) \right ) \\
$$

$$
= \frac { \partial ^ { k _ { l } - 2 } } { \partial \alpha _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } - 2 } } \left ( U _ { l } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { l } ) i [ H _ { l } , i [ H _ { l } , A _ { l + 1 } ] ] U _ { l } ( \alpha _ { l } ) \right ) \\
$$

$$
= \dots
$$

$$
= i ^ { k _ { l } } U _ { l } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { l } ) \underbrace { [ H _ { l } , \dots , [ H _ { l } , A _ { l + 1 } ] \dots ] U _ { l } ( \alpha _ { l } ) } _ { k _ { l } \text { times} } \, .
$$

From this result and the unitarily invariance properties of the norm, we have

$$
\left \| \frac { \partial ^ { k _ { l } } } { \partial \alpha _ { l } ^ { k _ { l } } } \left ( U _ { l } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { l } ) V _ { l } ^ { \dagger } A _ { l + 1 } V _ { l } U _ { l } ( \alpha _ { l } ) \right ) \right \| _ { \infty } & = \left \| [ H _ { l } , \dots , [ H _ { l } , V _ { l } ^ { \dagger } A _ { l + 1 } V _ { l } ] \dots ] \right \| _ { \infty } \\ & < ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| , \ k _ { l } \| V _ { l } ^ { \dagger } A _ { l } , \dots V _ { l } \| )
$$

$$
= ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { l } } \| A _ { l + 1 } \| _ { \infty } \, ,
$$

$$
& \ll ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { l } } \left \| V _ { l } ^ { \dagger } A _ { l + 1 } V _ { l } \right \| _ { \infty } \\ & \quad = ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { l } } \| A _ { l + 1 } \|
$$

where the last equality follows from unitarily invariance of the norm, and the inequality is obtained by induction using the property that for any operators A and B, we have

$$
\| [ A , B ] \| _ { \infty } & \leqslant \| A B \| _ { \infty } + \| B A \| _ { \infty } & ( C 1 3 3 ) \\ & \leqslant 2 \| A \| \ \| B \|
$$

$$
\leqslant 2 \| A \| _ { \infty } \| B \| _ { \infty } \, ,
$$

where we first use triangle inequality, and then sub-multiplicativity of the spectral norm. Now, using Eq. (C132) with Al = ∂ kl ∂αkl l (U † l (αl)V † l (... ∂ km ∂αkm m (U †m(αm)V †mOVmUm(αm)) ...) VlUl(αl)) gives ∥Al∥∞ ⩽ (2∥Hl∥∞)kl ∥Al+1∥∞. Finally, using it recursively starting from Eq. (C125) leads to the desired result.

$$
\| \partial ^ { k } \left ( ( U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) ) \| _ { \infty } & = \| A _ { 1 } \| _ { \infty } & ( C 1 3 5 ) \\ & < ( 2 \| H _ { \cdot } \| \, \| \, \lambda _ { 1 } \| _ { \Lambda _ { \cdot } } \| } ,
$$

$$
\leqslant \dots
$$

$$
& \leqslant ( 2 \| H _ { 1 } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { 1 } } \| A _ { 2 } \| _ { \infty } & & ( C 1 3 6 ) \\ & \leqslant & & ( C 1 3 7 )
$$

$$
\leqslant \| O \| _ { \infty } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { l } } \ ,
$$

where we used the convention that Am+1 = O in the last step. Now, we can simply upper bound the norm of each generator by the largest one, i.e., by maxl ∥Hl∥∞ which finally leads to

$$
| \partial ^ { k } f ( \alpha ) | \leqslant & \| O \| _ { \infty } \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } ( 2 \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } ) ^ { k _ { l } } & & ( C 1 3 9 )
$$

$$
\leqslant \| O \| _ { \infty } \left ( 2 \max _ { l } \| H _ { l } \| _ { \infty } \right ) ^ { k } \, .
$$

Finally, we can prove Corollary 2 as follows.

Proof. Now, from the assumption that each generator has a spectral norm of order O(1), then there exist a constant γ ∈ O(1) such that 2∥Hl∥∞ ⩽ γ for each l. Therefore, we recover Assumption 1 using Proposition 4, which allows us to apply Supplemental Theorem 3. Therefore, the bounded assumption for the observable i.e. ∥O∥∞ ∈ O(1) together with Supplemental Theorem 3 completes the proof of the corollary.

## 7. Application to circuits with Pauli generators

In this appendix we apply our surrogation technique to the special case when the circuit generators are Pauli strings. Furthermore, we will also explore the connection between the Taylor expansion based surrogate at the heart of Theorem 1 and the Pauli propagation surrogation we will discuss in Appendix E. Concretely, we will focus on a circuit with the following general structure

$$
U \left ( \alpha \right ) = \prod _ { j = 1 } ^ { m } V _ { j } U _ { j } ( \alpha _ { j } ) \, ,
$$

where Vj are a set of fixed unitary matrices (but not necessarily Clifford) and Uj(αj) = e-i αj 2 Pj are parametrized rotations with Pj Pauli strings. We will further suppose that the parameters αj are uncorrelated.

Under the previous constraints, we first can compute the Fourier expansion of the expectation function. Indeed, as it will be explicitly shown below (Appendix C7a), the expectation function can be naturally expressed in terms of sine/cosine functions as

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { \omega \in \{ 0 , 1 , 2 \} ^ { m } } \Upsilon _ { \omega } ( \delta ) \, \text {Tr} [ O _ { \omega } ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \rho _ { 0 } ] \, , \quad \text { (C142)}
$$

where δ = α-α∗ with α ∈ Vm(α∗ , r) a hypercube of the form of Eq. (3) sampled uniformly as in Eq. (4). Here ω = (ω1, ω2, ..., ωm) is an index vector with each ωj taking a value of 0, 1 or 2 and the Fourier coefficients are given by

$$
\Upsilon _ { \omega } ( \delta ) = \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } v _ { \omega _ { j } } ( \delta _ { j } ) \quad \text {where} ,
$$

$$
v _ { \omega _ { j } } ( \delta _ { j } ) = \begin{cases} \cos ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 0 \, , \\ 2 \sin \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) \cos \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 1 \, , \\ \sin ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 2 \, . \end{cases}
$$

The operators Oω(α∗ ) are defined recursively and can be computed efficiently. The recursive method to obtain them is detailed in Eq. (C173) in Appendix C7a.

For an individual term in the sum associated with ω, denote n1(ω) and n2(ω) as the number of times ωj = 1 and ωj = 2 appear in a given ω. Then we can define the order of each term as

$$
K ( \omega ) = n _ { 1 } ( \omega ) + 2 n _ { 2 } ( \omega ) \, .
$$

Notice that this count the number of sine contributions to the corresponding coefficient. We can then consider an order κ surrogate of the expectation function that is built by keeping only terms with K(ω) ⩽ κ. That is, we have

$$
\widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \coloneqq \sum _ { \omega \, ; \, K ( \omega ) \leqslant \kappa } \Upsilon _ { \omega } ( \delta ) \, T r [ O _ { \omega } ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \rho _ { 0 } ] \, .
$$

This surrogate is roughly equivalent to the Taylor surrogate (i.e., Eq. (C6)). Indeed, for a given order, the error of the two techniques. Trivially, we can compare the surrogate via Taylor and the Taylor expansion of the sin/ cos surrogate. We see that these are equivalent, as the terms dropped in the sin/ cos surrogate are also dropped in the Taylor one (this comes from the fact that cosα ∼ 1, sinα ∼ α). Therefore, the only difference between the two surrogate methods lies in the difference between the term Υ(δ) and its Taylor expansion at order κ. This difference is of order κ+1.

Now that we have established the connection between the Taylor and the sin / cos surrogation techniques, we devote the rest of this section to explaining how the latter connects with the Pauli Propagation technique. To do so, let us start by assuming that the measurement operator is given by a single Pauli, i.e., O = P (we will later generalize to the case of O = ∑i aiPi.). Furthermore, let us now assume that the Vi gates are Clifford gates.

Pauli Propagation (which we will detail further in Appendix E) is understood in the Heisenberg picture. Here, we study the backwards evolved measurement operator

$$
U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) P U ( \alpha ) = \prod _ { j = 1 , l = 1 } ^ { m } V _ { j } ^ { \dagger } U _ { j } ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha _ { j } ) P U _ { l } ( \alpha _ { l } ) V _ { l } \, .
$$

Let us start by using Eq. (C157) to compute the effect of applying the layer closest to the Pauli observable. For a general observable this would look like

$$
e ^ { i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } P e ^ { - i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } = v _ { 0 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) P + v _ { 1 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) \frac { i } { 2 } [ P _ { m } , P ] + v _ { 2 } ( \alpha ) P _ { m } P P _ { m } \, .
$$

However, Pauli strings either commute or anti-commute. Therefore, we can use this to our advantage. Indeed, if PmP = PPm we can trivially simplify the previous expression to

$$
e ^ { i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } P e ^ { - i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } = [ v _ { 0 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) + v _ { 2 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) ] \, P = P \, .
$$

Furthermore, in the case that PmP = -PPm, we can also simplify the terms in Eq. (C148) using i2 [Pm, P] = iPmP and PmPPm = -P to give

$$
e ^ { i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } P e ^ { - i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } = [ v _ { 0 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) - v _ { 2 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) ] \, P + v _ { 1 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) i P _ { m } P \, ,
$$

which by simple trigonometric identities (and by recalling Eq. (C144)) can be simplified to v0(αm)-v2(αm) = cos(αm), and v1(αm) = sin(αm). This means that Eq. (C148) can be rewritten as

$$
e ^ { i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } P e ^ { - i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } = \begin{cases} P & \text {if } [ P _ { m } , P ] = 0 \\ \cos ( \alpha _ { m } ) P + \sin ( \alpha _ { m } ) P ^ { \prime } & \text {if } \{ P _ { m } , P \} \end{cases}
$$

where P ′ := iPmP is a Pauli matrix (up to a sign). We can now define the Pauli Path coefficients defined in Eq. (E19) that we rewrite here:

$$
\phi _ { \omega _ { i } } ( \alpha _ { i } ) = \begin{cases} 1 , & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = 0 \\ \cos ( \alpha _ { i } ) , & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = 1 \\ \sin ( \alpha _ { i } ) , & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = - 1 \ . \end{cases}
$$

Thus we obtain

$$
e ^ { i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } P e ^ { - i \frac { \alpha _ { m } } { 2 } P _ { m } } = \begin{cases} \phi _ { 0 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) P = P & \text {iff } [ P _ { m } , P ] = 0 \\ \phi _ { 1 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) P + \phi _ { 2 } ( \alpha _ { m } ) P ^ { \prime } & \text {iff } \{ P _ { m } , P \} \end{cases}
$$

Now it is worth noting that when we apply any Clifford gate into any Pauli matrix, it returns a Pauli matrix. Therefore, we can repeat this procedure for all the circuit in Eq. (C147) to obtain

$$
U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) P U ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { \omega } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) P _ { \omega } \, ,
$$

where Pω are the propagated Pauli matrices. In other words, the final Pauli matrices obtained after applying the commutation rules for all of the gates in the circuit.

Finally, it is worth stressing that if O = ∑i aiPi instead of a single Pauli, due to the linearity of the expectation function the results still apply. Indeed, if we start from such observable, the expectation function can be written as

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \text {Tr} \left [ \rho U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) O U ( \alpha ) \right ] = \sum _ { i } a _ { i } \text {Tr} \left [ \rho U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) P _ { i } U ( \alpha ) \right ] ,
$$

and we can apply the propagation technique for each Pi. This previous leads to

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { i } \sum _ { \omega } a _ { i } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) \, \text {Tr} [ \rho P _ { \omega } ] = \sum _ { \omega } c _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) \, \text {Tr} [ \rho P _ { \omega } ] \, ,
$$

where we have introduced the notation cω(α) = ∑i aiΦω(α).

As the error associated with this Pauli propagation surrogation strategy are of the same order as the Taylor surrogate (i.e., Eq. (C6)), Theorem 1 can be used to indirectly bound the efficiency of Pauli Propagation. However, substantially tighter bounds can be obtained by analysing Pauli propagation directly as we will proceed to do in the next full appendix (Appendix E).

## a. Fourier expansion

Let us now study the Fourier expansion in Eq. (C142). We will explicitly show that the expectation function can naturally be expressed in terms of sine and cosine functions. In particular, since P 2 j = ✶, we find

$$
e ^ { - i \frac { \alpha _ { j } } { 2 } P _ { j } } = \cos \left ( \frac { \alpha _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) \mathbb { I } - i \sin \left ( \frac { \alpha _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) P _ { j } \, .
$$

In addition, the rotation gates can be re-expressed as perturbations α around any point α ∈ Vm(α∗ , r) for all j. This can be trivially seen by just writing α = α∗ + δ

$$
U \left ( \alpha \right ) = \prod _ { j = 1 } ^ { m } U _ { j } ( \delta _ { j } ) U _ { j } ( \alpha _ { j } ^ { * } ) V _ { j }
$$

$$
= \prod _ { j = 1 } ^ { m } U _ { j } ( \delta _ { j } ) \widetilde { V } _ { j } \, ,
$$

where the first equality holds due to e-i αj 2 Pj = e-i α∗j 2 Pj e-i δj 2 Pj and in the second equality we denote Ṽj := Vje-i α∗j 2 Pj . Also, we introduce the notation

$$
\rho _ { l } = \prod _ { j = 1 } ^ { l } U _ { j } ( \alpha _ { j } ) \widetilde { V } _ { j } \, \rho _ { 0 } \left ( \prod _ { j = 1 } ^ { l } U _ { j } ( \alpha _ { j } ) \widetilde { V } _ { j } \right ) ^ { \dagger }
$$

Now, the expectation function as defined in Eq. (1) can be expanded as

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \text { Tr} \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } \tilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } U _ { m } ^ { \dagger } ( \delta _ { m } ) O U _ { m } ( \delta _ { m } ) \tilde { V } _ { m } \right ] \\
$$

$$
= \text {Tr} \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } \widetilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } \left ( \cos \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \mathbb { 1 } + i \sin \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) P _ { m } \right ) O \left ( \cos \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \mathbb { 1 } - i \sin \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) P _ { m } \right ) \widetilde { V } _ { m } \right ]
$$

$$
= \cos ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \text {Tr} \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } \left ( \widetilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } O \widetilde { V } _ { m } \right ) \right ] + 2 \sin \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \cos \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \text {Tr} \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } \left ( \widetilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } \frac { i } { 2 } \left [ P _ { m } , O \right ] \widetilde { V } _ { m } \right ) \right ]
$$

$$
+ \sin ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) T r \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } \left ( \widetilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } P _ { m } O P _ { m } \widetilde { V } _ { m } \right ) \right ]
$$

$$
= v _ { 0 } ( \delta _ { m } ) \, T r \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } O _ { 0 } \right ] + v _ { 1 } ( \delta _ { m } ) \, T r \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } O _ { 1 } \right ] + v _ { 2 } ( \delta _ { m } ) \, T r \left [ \rho _ { m - 1 } O _ { 2 } \right ] \, ,
$$

where we defined

and

$$
O _ { 0 } = & \widetilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } O \widetilde { V } _ { m } \\
$$

$$
O _ { 1 } = & \widetilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } \, \frac { i } { 2 } \left [ P _ { m } , O \right ] \widetilde { V } _ { m } \\ & \widetilde { \sim } _ { \dagger } \, \frac { \widetilde { \sim } _ { m } } { 2 } \, \widetilde { \sim } _ { m }
$$

$$
O _ { 3 } = & \widetilde { V } _ { m } ^ { \dagger } P _ { m } O P _ { m } \widetilde { V } _ { m } \\
$$

$$
v _ { 0 } ( \delta _ { m } ) = \cos ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \\
$$

$$
v _ { 1 } ( \delta _ { m } ) = & 2 \sin \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \cos \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \\
$$

$$
v _ { 2 } ( \delta _ { m } ) = \sin ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { m } } { 2 } \right ) \, .
$$

By iteratively repeating the previous steps, we obtain the form of the expectation function by Fourier expansion as

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { \omega \in \{ 0 , 1 , 2 \} ^ { m } } \Upsilon _ { \omega } ( \delta ) \text { Tr} [ O _ { \omega } ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \rho _ { 0 } ] \, , \quad \text { (C172) }
$$

where ω = (ω1, ω2, ..., ωm) is an index vector with each ωj taking a value of 0, 1 or 2. We also introduce a notation ω(i,j) = (ωi, ωi+1, ..., ωj) with i ⩽ j and ω = ω(1,m). Now, the operators Oω(1,m) := Oω(α∗ ) are defined recursively by

$$
O _ { \omega _ { ( j , m ) } } = \begin{cases} \tilde { V } _ { j } ^ { \dagger } O _ { \omega _ { ( j + 1 , m ) } } \tilde { V } _ { j } & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 0 \, , \\ \frac { i } { 2 } \tilde { V } _ { j } ^ { \dagger } \left [ P _ { l } , O _ { \omega _ { ( i + 1 , m ) } } \right ] \tilde { V } _ { j } & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 1 \, , \\ \tilde { V } _ { j } ^ { \dagger } P _ { j } O _ { \omega _ { ( j + 1 , m ) } } P _ { j } \tilde { V } _ { j } & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 2 \, , \end{cases}
$$

with Ṽj = e-i α∗j 2 Pj Vj, as defined above, and the Fourier coefficients are given by Υω(δ) = ∏mj=1 υωj (δj), where

$$
v _ { \omega _ { j } } ( \delta _ { j } ) = \begin{cases} \cos ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 0 \, , \\ 2 \sin \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) \cos \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 1 \, , \\ \sin ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { \delta _ { j } } { 2 } \right ) & \text {if } \omega _ { j } = 2 \, . \end{cases}
$$

## Appendix D: Iterative training strategy with patch surrogate

In this section, we study an iterative training procedure for parameterized quantum circuits based on patch surrogate. Here, instead of employing a standard hybrid classical-quantum optimization strategy where a loss gradient is estimated at each training iteration, we construct a surrogate of loss gradients around a patch and then perform several classical gradient steps within this patch. After few training iterations, we need to reset the surrogate around a new patch. We investigate whether this iterative strategy can be advantageous. The section is structured as follows:

- In Appendix D1, we provide a detailed description of the iterative surrogate approach.
- In Appendix D2, we numerically compare the surrogate with the standard gradient descent.
- In Appendix D3, further numerical details are provided including a detailed description of our shot allocation strategy.
- Lastly, in Appendix D4, the analytical results relating shot noise and shot allocation are derived.

## 1. Description of a surrogate approach

We consider a quantum surrogate based strategy where the loss gradients are surrogated and then trained purely classically. More specifically, we consider a Taylor surrogate of the loss gradients ∇f(α) around a point α∗ and train this surrogate classically using gradient descent for a small number of steps. To ensure the faithfulness of the training, the process of re-surrogating the loss gradients is then repeated using the newly obtained point as the next expansion center.

For simplicity, we consider a second-order Taylor expansion of the gradient of the form

$$
\nabla \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) & = \nabla f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + H ( \alpha ^ { * } ) ( \alpha - \alpha ^ { * } ) \ , \\ f ( \alpha ) & = \Delta ^ { 2 } \cdot \Delta ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + H ( \alpha ^ { * } ) ( \alpha - \alpha ^ { * } ) \ ,
$$

where H(α∗ ) is the Hessian of the loss at the expansion point. The individual components read:

$$
\partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) = \partial _ { l } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { m } \partial _ { l , k } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \, \delta _ { k } , \quad \delta _ { k } = \alpha _ { k } - \alpha _ { k } ^ { * } .
$$

We note that it is straightforward to extend this construction to higher-order surrogate models. Algorithm 1 summarizes the overall protocol. Moreover, whereas other parts of our work primarily focus on surrogating the loss itself, in practical gradient-based training it is often more appropriate to surrogate the loss gradient directly. A related strategy, termed Quantum Analytic Descent, was introduced in Ref. [31]; it is based on a second-order truncation of the sine coefficients in the Fourier expansion (see Appendix C7a).

Since standard GD is unbiased as opposed to the surrogate strategy which suffers from Taylor error, we are interested in comparing the performance on both methods by fixing an equivalent total shot budget. This is obtained by choosing

$$
N _ { g r a d } \approx \frac { N _ { s u r r o g a t e } } { N _ { i t e r s } } \ ,
$$

where Ngrad is the number of measurement shots for one iteration of standard GD, Niters is the total number of training iterations for standard GD and Nsurrogate is the total number of measurement shots used in the surrogate training strategy. In this case, the shot noise with standard GD will increase by a factor Niters, the number of iterations between surrogate updates. We aim to study how, in the task of estimating the ground state of a given Hamiltonian, the additional shot noise incurred by standard gradient descent balances against the Taylor truncation error introduced by the surrogate, and how this trade-off impacts overall optimization performance.

## Algorithm 1 Iterative Training via Taylor Surrogate

```
Input: Initial parameters α1, number of surrogates Nsurr, steps per surrogate Niters
for i = 1 to Nsurr do
if i = 1 then
Set α∗ = α1
elseSet α∗ = αi

end
Construct Taylor surrogate ∇˜ f(α) around α∗ by estimating ∇f(α∗ ) and H(α∗ )
Perform Niters steps of classical gradient descent with ∇˜ f(α) starting from α∗
Let αi+1 be the final iterate
```

```
end
```

## 2. Summary of the results: Comparison study with standard gradient descent

In this section, we empirically investigate the performance of the surrogate approach and compare its resource consumption with that of the standard VQA approach.

We consider the task of estimating the ground state of a Hamiltonian. The target Hamiltonian is a rescaled Heisenberg chain given by

$$
O = \frac { 1 } { 3 ( n - 1 ) } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n - 1 } ( X _ { i } X _ { i + 1 } + Y _ { i } Y _ { i + 1 } + Z _ { i } Z _ { i + 1 } ) \, .
$$

The variational ansatz consists of L layers of single-qubit rotations followed by two-qubit ZZ gates of the form

$$
U ( \alpha ) = \prod _ { l = 1 } ^ { L } \left ( \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } R _ { Z Z } ( \alpha _ { i + 2 n , l } ) \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } R _ { Z } ( \alpha _ { i + n , l } ) R _ { X } ( \alpha _ { i , l } ) \right ) ,
$$

with |ψ(α)⟩ = U(α) |0⟩⊗n . The total number of parameters is m= 3nL.

We explore different strategies for allocating measurement shots for the surrogate; details are provided in Appendix D3 a. One approach is straightforward and easily generalizable to higher-order expansions, while the second is slightly more optimized for practical implementation. The key difference is that the optimized strategy evaluates each loss only once for repeated Hessian diagonal elements, whereas the naive approach recomputes the same loss separately for each diagonal element. In Appendix D3a, we also compare these methods with standard gradient descent under an equivalent total shot budget. Additionally, we employ a two-phase training schedule for the surrogate, where the number of iterations between surrogate updates, Niters, is adjusted once during the simulation (see Appendix D3b).

## b. Results discussion

Numerical details in simulations: Let Surrogate 1 and Surrogate 2 denote the naïve and the optimized surrogate strategies, respectively, and let standard GD denote standard gradient descent with an equivalent quantum resource budget. As detailed in Appendix D3a, our shot allocation is fully determined by fixing the number of shots per loss evaluation, F2 - that is, the total number of shots to compute the gradient is given by 2mF2. In our experiments, we set F2 = 600. During the first training phase, we use N (1) iters = 10 and N (1) surr = 20. In the second phase, we set N (2) iters = 8 and N (2) surr = 200, corresponding to a total of 1800 GD steps. At each GD step, the test loss is computed as the difference between the exact expectation value of the observable and the ground state energy obtained numerically using scipy.sparse.linalg.eigsh. All methods are run independently 10 times with different random initial parameters. Importantly, the three methods share the same initial parameters in each run. The learning rate is fixed at η = 0.1.

Results and Discussion: In Fig. 6, we show the test loss as a function of the number of shots (at depths (a) L = 3 and (c) L = 6) and the number of iterations ((b) L = 3 and (d) L = 6) for a 10-qubit Heisenberg Hamiltonian. We selected examples where the best final test loss is achieved either by standard GD (L = 6) or by Surrogate 2 (L = 3); however, the differences are small, particularly in terms of the interquartile range (IQR). We also compute the number of shots required to reduce the test loss below 10-1 for L = 2, 3, 4, 5, 6. The results are shown in Fig. 7 for n = 8 (a) and n = 10 (b) qubits. Again, there is no clear distinction in performance between the strategies.

The empirical results observed from Fig. 6 and Fig. 7 show that gradient descent (GD) and the surrogate-based approaches exhibit comparable performance in terms of quantum resource usage, although both methods achieve good accuracy for the tasks considered. Intuitively, while standard GD suffers from larger shot noise, its stochastically unbiased nature compensates for this drawback. Nevertheless, this does not rule out the possibility that more sophisticated surrogate approaches could yield a distinct performance advantage. Indeed, early evidence for a Fourierbased surrogate method has been reported in Ref. [31]. A plausible explanation is that such approaches preserve the periodicity of the loss function and retain partial contributions from higher-order perturbative terms, although this remains a hypothesis.

From a practical perspective, the key distinction is that GD consumes quantum resources at every step, whereas the surrogate expends a larger quantum budget up front and then reuses the resulting information across multiple iterations. This can be advantageous in two separate ways. First, it can better tolerate noise drift on real devices: by amortizing quantum evaluations into fewer, more substantial data-collection phases, it reduces the need to repeatedly query the hardware under slowly changing calibration conditions. As a result, successive optimization updates are less exposed to time-dependent variations in gate errors and measurement bias that can otherwise introduce inconsistent gradient estimates. Second, on cloud-based quantum platforms, it can mitigate the impact of variable queueing delays and inflated wall-clock runtimes that arise when jobs are repeatedly submitted.

## c. Further numerical experiment: Training inside a single patch

We now focus on a single surrogate and investigate how many GD steps can be performed from random initial parameters before the test loss begins to increase. The goal is to identify practically relevant values for Niters.

Simulation details. We fix F2 = 600 and η = 0.1 as in previous simulations and consider the naïve surrogate method. The gradient is surrogated around random initial parameters α∗ , and GD is performed classically until the test loss starts to increase. We record the maximal value of Niters for each run. We set n = L and perform nrun = 20 independent runs for each n = L ∈ {2, 3, . . . , 10}.

Results and discussion. The results are shown in Fig. 8, which plots the optimal number of iterations Niters as a function of the number of parameters m. The shaded area corresponds to the minimum-maximum range over all nrun = 20 random initializations, the diamond markers indicate the mean, and the solid line shows a power-law fit (linear fit in log-log scale). The minimal value observed is Niters = 9, suggesting that the choice of Niters = 8-10 in previous simulations was reasonable. These values were set heuristically based on preliminary tests. In practice, the test loss would not be accessible and so how to obtain the optimal Niters remains an open problem. The corresponding distances to the center of the patch are shown in Fig. 3 in the main text. One practical approach for selecting the number of iterations could be to restrict parameters within the patch of radius r ∈ O(1/√m), where theoretical guarantees exist. However, Fig. 3 indicates that the surrogate can be trained beyond this patch, meaning such a restriction would be suboptimal.
