## 3. Further numerical details for Taylor surrogate

## a. Shot allocation in simulation

To account for finite sampling noise, we assign a number of measurement shots N(δα) to each loss evaluation f(α∗ + δα). Instead of explicitly sampling loss evaluations according to the optimal randomized measurement distribution of Lemma 7, we adopt a deterministic shot-allocation scheme. Specifically, we approximate the optimal randomized measurement process by fixing the shot allocation to its average over the optimal sampling distribution, which preserves the same asymptotic scaling where the local patch size scales as r ∼ O(1/m) while being marginally simpler to implement numerically. We fix a reference number of shots F2 for gradient evaluations (i.e., for shifts of the form

± π2 el) and define the remaining allocations proportionally:

$$
F _ { 3 } \equiv \frac { F _ { 2 } } { m } , \quad ( Hessian - \text {related shifts} )
$$

$$
F _ { 1 } \equiv \frac { \stackrel { F _ { 2 } } { F _ { 2 } } } { \sqrt { m } } , \quad ( \text {central point evaluation} ) .
$$

This reflects the intuition that larger offsets or higher-order terms can be estimated more coarsely.

(1) Naïve strategy (without periodicity) In this straightforward approach, all loss evaluations required by the parameter-shift rules are treated independently. In particular, the Hessian is estimated using four separate evaluations for each element:

$$
f \left ( \alpha ^ { * } \pm \frac { \pi } { 2 } ( e _ { l } \pm e _ { k } ) \right ) , \ \ k , l = 1 , \dots , m .
$$

For k = l, this includes multiple evaluations of f(α∗ ). Denoting Nl,i(0) as the number of shots used for the two independent central-point evaluations corresponding to the l-th diagonal term (i = 1, 2), we set Nl,i(0) = F3 for simplicity. The total number of shots required to construct one surrogate is then:

$$
N _ { s u r g o r a t e } ^ { n a i v e } = 2 m F _ { 2 } + 4 \cdot \frac { m ( m + 1 ) } { 2 } F _ { 3 } & & ( D 9 ) \\ & & 0
$$

$$
= 2 m F _ { 2 } + 2 m ( m + 1 ) F _ { 3 } .
$$

$$
N _ { s u r r o g a t e } ^ { n a i v e } = ( 4 m + 2 ) F _ { 2 } .
$$

(2) Optimized strategy (with periodicity) By exploiting the periodicity property

$$
f ( \alpha + 2 \pi e _ { l } ) = f ( \alpha ) ,
$$

the diagonal Hessian elements can be computed more efficiently:

$$
\partial _ { l } ^ { 2 } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) = \frac { f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \pi e _ { l } ) - f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) } { 2 } .
$$

Each diagonal element requires only one additional loss evaluation, while the central point f(α∗ ) is reused across all terms. The total number of shots per surrogate is then

$$
N _ { s u r g o r a t e } ^ { o p t } = F _ { 1 } + 2 m F _ { 2 } + F _ { 3 } \left [ m + 4 \cdot \frac { m ( m - 1 ) } { 2 } \right ]
$$

$$
= F _ { 1 } + 2 m F _ { 2 } + F _ { 3 } m ( 2 m - 1 ) .
$$

Substituting F1 = F2/√m and F3 = F2/m gives

$$
N _ { s u r g o g a t e } ^ { o p t } = \left ( 4 m - 1 + \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { m } } \right ) F _ { 2 } .
$$

Summary Both strategies scale similarly as O(mF2), but the optimized strategy is slightly more shot-efficient due to reuse of the central evaluation and compression of diagonal Hessian evaluations. Nevertheless, the naive approach can slightly reduce shot noise in practice, resulting in comparable performance in terms of quantum resources.

Standard gradient descent Standard gradient descent requires estimating the gradient at each iteration, which involves 2m loss evaluations via the parameter-shift rule. Let F GD 2 denote the number of shots per evaluation. To ensure a fair comparison with the surrogate methods under an equivalent total shot budget, we set

$$
F _ { 2 } ^ { G D } \approx \frac { N _ { \text {sugarate} } ^ { \text {nive} } } { 2 m N _ { \text {iters} } } = \frac { ( 2 + \frac { 1 } { m } ) \, F _ { 2 } } { N _ { \text {iters} } } .
$$

Substituting F3 = F2/m gives

## b. Two-phase surrogate schedule

We adopt a two-phase training schedule in which the frequency of surrogate updates changes over the course of optimization. Empirically, this heuristic improves the performance of the surrogate. Concretely, we define:

- N (1) surr: number of surrogate refreshes in the first phase,
- N (1) iters : number of classical gradient steps between surrogates in the first phase,
- N (2) surr: number of surrogate refreshes in the second phase,
- N (2) iters : number of classical gradient steps between surrogates in the second phase,

with the typical choice

$$
N _ { i t e r s } ^ { ( 1 ) } > N _ { i t e r s } ^ { ( 2 ) } , \ N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 1 ) } < N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 2 ) } .
$$

This adaptive schedule balances exploration and local accuracy: larger patches in the early phase encourage movement out of flat regions, while smaller patches in the later phase ensure the Taylor surrogate remains accurate near convergence.

When comparing the surrogate strategy to standard gradient descent under an equivalent total shot budget, we need to adapt Eq. (D17) to account for the two different values of Niters. Specifically, we enforce

$$
N _ { s u r r o g a t e } ^ { n a i v e } \left ( N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 1 ) } + N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 2 ) } \right ) = 2 m F _ { 2 } ^ { G D 1 } \left ( N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 1 ) } N _ { i ters } ^ { ( 1 ) } + N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 2 ) } N _ { i ters } ^ { ( 2 ) } \right ) .
$$

Since this equation may yield a non-integer value for F GD 2 , we round it as

$$
F _ { 2 } ^ { G D } = \left \lfloor ( 2 + 1 / m ) F _ { 2 } \frac { N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 1 ) } + N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 2 ) } } { N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 1 ) } N _ { i ters } ^ { ( 1 ) } + N _ { s u r r } ^ { ( 2 ) } N _ { i ters } ^ { ( 2 ) } } \right \rfloor .
$$

This is equivalent to Eq. (D17) with Niters replaced by an effective average over the simulation.

## 4. Analytical guarantees

We now analyze how shot noise propagates through the surrogate-based gradient estimation (not the shot noise analysis for the Taylor surrogate of the loss directly was covered in Appendix D2). To understand the impact of statistical uncertainty, we construct a Taylor expansion of the surrogate gradient ∇f̃(α) around a chosen expansion point α∗ :

$$
\nabla \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) & = \nabla f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + H ( \alpha ^ { * } ) ( \alpha - \alpha ^ { * } ) \ , \\ \\ \intertext { t h e r } \nabla \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) & = \nabla f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + H ( \alpha ^ { * } ) ( \alpha - \alpha ^ { * } ) \ , \\
$$

where H(α∗ ) represents the Hessian of the loss function. Decomposing this into individual components for each parameter l, we obtain:

$$
\partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) = \partial _ { l } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { m } \partial _ { l , k } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) \, \delta _ { k } , \quad \delta _ { k } = \alpha _ { k } - \alpha _ { k } ^ { * } .
$$

To estimate these derivatives on a quantum processor, we employ the parameter-shift rule. This approach allows us to compute exact gradients and second-order derivatives by evaluating the circuit at specific shifts:

$$
\partial _ { l } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) & = \frac { f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) - f ( \alpha ^ { * } - \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) } { 2 } , \\ f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } - \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } - \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } ) ) + \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } + \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } ) + \pi ( \alpha ^ { * } ) ) }
$$

$$
\partial _ { l , k } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) = \frac { f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \frac { \pi } { 2 } ( e _ { l } + e _ { k } ) ) - f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \frac { \pi } { 2 } ( e _ { l } - e _ { k } ) ) - f ( \alpha ^ { * } - \frac { \pi } { 2 } ( e _ { l } - e _ { k } ) ) + f ( \alpha ^ { * } - \frac { \pi } { 2 } ( e _ { l } + e _ { k } ) ) } { 4 } .
$$

We define the shot noise error on the full gradient vector as the expected L2-norm distance between the empirical estimate and the exact surrogate gradient (its mean):

$$
\mathbb { E } \left [ \left \| \nabla \tilde { f } ( \alpha ) - \mathbb { E } \left [ \nabla \tilde { f } ( \alpha ) \right ] \right \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right ] = \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \text {Var} [ \partial _ { l } \tilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] \, .
$$

Assuming independent sampling noise for each evaluation, we propagate the variance through the parameter-shift expressions. Summing the contributions from the first-order shifts, the Hessian diagonals, and the off-diagonal crossterms, we obtain

$$
\sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } V a [ \partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] = \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { V a [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) ] + V a [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } - \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) ] } { 4 } + \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { m } \delta _ { k } ^ { 2 } \frac { \sum _ { \pm , \pm } V a [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } \pm \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } \pm \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { k } ) ] } { 1 6 } \ .
$$

In the following, we perform the shot-noise analysis over these different scenarios:

- Periodicity shot allocation for patch surrogate
- Naive shot allocation for patch surrogate, without periodicity,
- Comparison to standard gradient descents.

## a. Periodicity shot allocation for patch surrogate

Here, we analyze the shot allocation in Eq. (D25) using the periodicity condition. That is, by utilizing the periodicity of the loss, f(α+2πek) = f(α), we can simplify the diagonal elements of the Hessian, reducing the number of required quantum circuit evaluations:

$$
\partial _ { l } ^ { 2 } f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) = \frac { f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \pi e _ { l } ) - f ( \alpha ^ { * } ) } { 2 } .
$$

Let N(δα) be the number of shots allocated for measuring f(α∗ + δα). The variance of the estimate is bounded by the single-shot variance:

$$
V a r [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \delta \alpha ) ] = \frac { V a r _ { 1 } [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \delta \alpha ) ] } { N ( \delta \alpha ) } \leqslant \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } } { N ( \delta \alpha ) } \, ,
$$

where Var1[·] is upper-bounded by the squared norm of the observable O:

$$
Var _ { 1 } [ f ( \alpha ) ] & = \langle \psi ( \alpha ) | O ^ { 2 } \, | \psi ( \alpha ) \rangle - \langle \psi ( \alpha ) | O \, | \psi ( \alpha ) \rangle ^ { 2 } \\ & \leqslant \langle \psi ( \alpha ) | O ^ { 2 } \, | \psi ( \alpha ) \rangle \\ & \leqslant \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } \, .
$$

By inserting Eq. (D27) and assuming a maximum patch radius |δl| ⩽ r into Eq. (D25), we find:

$$
\frac { \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \, V a _ { [ \partial l } \tilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] } { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } } & \leqslant \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \left ( \frac { 1 } { 4 N ( \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) } + \frac { 1 } { 4 N ( - \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) } + \frac { r ^ { 2 } } { 4 N ( \pi e _ { l } ) } \right ) + \frac { m r ^ { 2 } } { 4 N ( 0 ) } \\ & + \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \sum _ { k = l + 1 } ^ { m } \frac { r ^ { 2 } } { 8 } \left ( \sum _ { \pm , \pm } \frac { 1 } { N ( \pm \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } \pm e _ { k } ) ) } \right ) \, .
$$

Applying the shot allocation (F1, F2, F3) denoted in Appendix D3a, the expression simplifies to:

$$
\frac { \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } V a [ \partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] } { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } } & \leqslant \frac { m } { 2 F _ { 2 } } + \frac { m ^ { 2 } r ^ { 2 } } { 4 F _ { 3 } } + \frac { m r ^ { 2 } } { 4 F _ { 1 } } = \frac { m } { 2 F _ { 2 } } \left ( 1 + \frac { m ^ { 2 } r ^ { 2 } } { 2 } + \frac { \sqrt { m } r ^ { 2 } } { 2 } \right ) \, .
$$

Considering a scaling of r ∈ O(1/m), the shot noise scales as:

$$
\sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } V a r [ \partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } m } { F _ { 2 } } \right ) = \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } m ^ { 2 } } { N _ { s u r r o g a t e } ^ { o p t i } } \right ) \, .
$$

## b. Naive shot allocation for patch surrogate without periodicity

If we ignore periodicity and recompute the loss at the fixed point α∗ whenever it is required, the diagonal Hessian elements require 4 independent evaluations each. In this naive case, the total variance becomes:

$$
\sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } V a r [ \partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] = \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \frac { V a r [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } + \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) ] + V a r [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } - \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } ) ] } { 4 } + \sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { m } \delta _ { k } ^ { 2 } \cdot \frac { \sum _ { \pm } \sum _ { \pm } V a r [ f ( \alpha ^ { * } \pm \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { l } \pm \frac { \pi } { 2 } e _ { k } ) ] } { 1 6 } .
$$

Applying the same bounds and the allocation from Appendix D3a, the error bound is:

$$
\sum _ { \frac { m } { l = 1 } } ^ { m } \text {Var} [ \partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] & \leqslant \frac { m } { 2 F _ { 2 } } + \frac { m ^ { 2 } r ^ { 2 } } { 4 F _ { 3 } } = \frac { m } { 2 F _ { 2 } } \left ( 1 + \frac { m ^ { 2 } r ^ { 2 } } { 2 } \right ) \, . \\ \intertext { d s i c h t h t y l o w r $ t h o t o l $ w h a r $ f o r a w i r o w $ i s c h i n f i f e n t l y $ b i c h o r $ T h u s $ f o r $ w $ $ n $ $ 1 / m $ $ t h o }
$$

While the bound is slightly lower, the total number of shots required is significantly higher. Thus, for r ∼ 1/m, the shot noise maintains the same asymptotic scaling:

$$
\sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } V a [ \partial _ { l } \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) ] \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } m ^ { 2 } } { N _ { s u r r o g a t e } ^ { n a i v e } } \right ) \, .
$$

## c. Comparison to standard GD

For standard gradient descent (GD), we assume that each of the 2m loss evaluations for the gradient is estimated using F GD 2 shots. The total variance is upper-bounded by:

$$
\sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } V a r [ \partial _ { l } f ( \alpha ) ] \leqslant \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } m } { 2 F _ { 2 } ^ { G D } } \ .
$$

Recalling that F GD 2 ≈ 2 Niters F2, we see that the variance for a standard gradient evaluation is approximately Niters/2 times larger than that of the surrogate gradient:

$$
\sum _ { l = 1 } ^ { m } V a r [ \partial _ { l } f ( \alpha ) ] \in \mathcal { O } \left ( \frac { \| O \| _ { \infty } ^ { 2 } m N _ { i ters } } { F _ { 2 } } \right ) \, .
$$

## Appendix E: Small angle Pauli Propagation

This section is devoted to the classical simulation and surrogation of quantum circuits in the near-Clifford regime.

## 1. The Pauli Propagation framework

We start by briefly reviewing the Pauli-path formalism and how it can employed for classically estimating expectation values of quantum circuits. For further details, see also Refs. [2, 14, 26, 112] and references therein.

Let U(α) be a quantum circuit written as parameterized perturbations from Clifford circuits. That is, of the form

$$
U ( \alpha ) = \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } C _ { i } U _ { i } ( \alpha _ { i } ) \, ,
$$

where {Ci}mi=1 are a set of fixed Clifford operators and {Ui(αi) = e-iαiPi/2}mi=1 are parameterized rotations generated by the Pauli operators {Pi}mi=1 .

Given an observable O = ∑P∈Pn aPP and an initial state ρ, we want to approximate the following expectation value:

$$
f ( \alpha ) = \text {Tr} \left [ O U ( \alpha ) \rho U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) \right ] .
$$

For the sake of simplicity, we will consider observables consisting of a single Pauli term. In the following lemma, we show that this assumption comes with no loss of generality.

Lemma 8 (Transferring guarantees from Pauli observables). Let S ⊆ {I,X, Y,Z}⊗n be a subset of Pauli operators and let O = ∑P∈S aPP be an observable.

1. Worst-case error. For all P ∈ S let f̃ (P) (α) be a function satisfying

$$
\left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } \right ] \right | \leqslant \epsilon .
$$

Then the function f̃(α) = ∑P∈S aP f̃ (P) (α) satisfies the following

$$
\left | \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | \leqslant \| a \| _ { 1 } \epsilon .
$$

2. Mean Square Error. Given a distribution D over [-π, π]m, for all P ∈ S let f̃ (P) (α) be a function satisfying

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } \right ] \right | ^ { 2 } \leqslant \epsilon ^ { 2 } .
$$

Then the function f̃(α) = ∑P∈S aP f̃ (P) (α) satisfies the following

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left | \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | ^ { 2 } \leqslant \| a \| _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } \epsilon ^ { 2 } .
$$

Proof. The first part of the lemma follows by a simple application of the triangle inequality.

$$
\left | \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | & = \left | \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } a _ { P } \left ( \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] \right ) \right | \\ \prec \sum \nolimits _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } | \widetilde { f } ( P ) ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] | & \prec \| \Omega \| + \| \Omega \| + \\
$$

$$
\leq & \sum _ { P \in S } | a _ { P } | \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] \right | \leqslant \| a \| _ { 1 } \epsilon . \\
$$

As for the second part, we have

$$
\left | \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | ^ { 2 } = & \left | \sum _ { P \in S } a _ { P } \left ( \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } \right ] \right ) \right | ^ { 2 } \\ & \left ( 1 - 2 S \right )
$$

$$
\leqslant \left ( \sum | a _ { P } | \, \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } \right ] \right )
$$

$$
& \quad ( R \in S \\ & = \left ( \sum _ { P \in S } \sqrt { | a _ { P } | } \sqrt { | a _ { P } | } \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] \right | \right ) ^ { 2 } \\ & = \left ( \sum _ { P \in S } \left ( \sqrt { | a _ { P } | } \right ) \left ( - \right ) \left ( \frac { 1 } { 2 } + 1 \right ) \rho U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } \right ) ^ { 2 } \\
$$

$$
& | P \in \mathcal { S } \\ & \leqslant \left ( \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } | a _ { P } | \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - T r [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] \right | \right ) ^ { 2 } \\ & \quad \leqslant \left ( \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } | a _ { P } | \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - T r [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] \right | \right ) ^ { 2 }
$$

$$
\leq & \left ( \sum _ { P \in S } | a _ { P } | \right ) \left ( \sum _ { P \in S } | a _ { P } | \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] \right | ^ { 2 } \right ) \\
$$

$$
= & \| a \| _ { 1 } \left ( \sum _ { P \in S } | a _ { P } | \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } \right ] \right | ^ { 2 } \right ) , \\ \intertext { s u n d t h e i n g t r a l l i n g u l l i n t a n d i v e a l l i n t h e f u r t h e a t w h s s a n t h e i n g u l l i n t a t t h e s s a t h e s s a t t h e a t t h e s s a t t h e
$$

where in the second step we used the triangle inequality and in the fourth step we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality - i.e. (∑i xiyi)2 ⩽ (∑i x2i )(∑i y2 i ). We conclude the proof by taking the expectation over α.

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left | \widetilde { f } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | ^ { 2 } = & \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \mathbb { I } a _ { 1 } \left ( \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { S } } | a _ { P } | \Big | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } \right ] \right | ^ { 2 } \right ) \\
$$

$$
\left | \nabla P \in & s \quad \left | \nabla P ( \alpha ) \rho ( \alpha ) \right | ^ { 2 } \right \rangle \\ = & \| a \| _ { 1 } \left ( \sum _ { P \in S } | a _ { P } | \underbrace { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left | \widetilde { f } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - T r [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } ] \right | ^ { 2 } } _ { \leqslant 2 } \right ) \\ \leqslant & \| a \| _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } \epsilon ^ { 2 } , \\ \text {used the fact that the expectation is linear.}
$$

where in the second step we used the fact that the expectation is linear.

Thus, given a Pauli operator P , we consider the back-propagated (i.e., Heisenberg-evolved) observable U(α)†PU(α) and express it in the Pauli-path formalism

$$
U ( \alpha ) ^ { \dagger } P U ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { \omega } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) P _ { \omega } ,
$$

where ω ∈ {-1, 0, 1}m is the so-called frequency vector or path vector that uniquely enumerates a Pauli path, and Pω is the backpropagated Pauli along the path ω . If at the Pauli gate Ui in Eq. (5) the backpropagating Pauli operator commutes with with Pi, then ωi = 0. If it anticommutes, ωi = 1 or ωi = -1 depending on whether the Pauli path picked up a cosine or sine coefficient, respectively. Φω(α) thus represents the coefficient of the backpropagated Pauli path and is defined as

$$
\Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) = \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \phi _ { \omega _ { i } } ( \alpha _ { i } ) ,
$$

$$
\phi _ { \omega _ { 1 } } ( \alpha _ { i } ) = \begin{cases} 1 , & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = 0 \\ \cos ( \alpha _ { i } ) , & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = 1 \quad . \\ \sin ( \alpha _ { i } ) , & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = - 1 \end{cases} .
$$

$$
d _ { \omega } \colon = & \text { Tr} [ \rho P _ { \omega } ] \in [ - 1 , 1 ] \\ & \text { Tr} [ \rho P _ { \omega } ] = 0
$$

as the overlap of the backpropagated Pauli along the path ω with the initial state, and we denote the expectation function as

$$
f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) = \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) \right ] = \sum _ { \omega } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } .
$$

$$
\mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } \coloneqq \{ \ p a t h s \, \omega | \, \omega \, \text { contains more than } \kappa \, \text { negative entries} \} \subseteq \{ - 1 , 0 , 1 \} ^ { m } \\ \quad \ \ r _ { \kappa } \, \quad \ \dot { \omega } _ { 1 } \, \colon = \, \{ \ p a t h s \, \omega | \, \omega \, \text { contains more than } \kappa \, \text { negative entries} \} \subseteq \{ - 1 , 0 , 1 \} ^ { m } \\
$$

We additionally defineS m

as the set of paths ω with more than κ entries of -1, i.e., the set of paths ω with more than κ sine coefficients. For simplicity, we define

$$
\# \sin ( \omega ) \, \colon = \, \text {number of negative entries of } \omega \, ,
$$

which is also the number of sine coefficients of the path ω. Finally, we introduce the truncated function f̃ (P ) κ (α) which contains only the terms with at most κ sines

$$
\widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \coloneqq \sum _ { \omega \not \in S _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } \ . \\
$$

Similarly, we will use the notation P̃(α) to denote the truncated version of the back-propagated operator P given by

$$
\widetilde { P } ( \alpha ) \coloneqq \sum _ { \omega \not \in S _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) P _ { \omega } \ . \\
$$

In the following, we will consider two regimes:

- Average-case. When the parameter vector α is equipped with a suitable distribution D over the hypercube Vm(0, r), we consider the mean square error

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left [ \left ( f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] = \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left [ \left ( \sum _ { \omega \in S _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] .
$$

- Worst-case. When the parameter vector α is an arbitrarily chosen point in the hypercube Vm(0, r), we consider the worst-case error

$$
\sup _ { \nu _ { m } ( 0 , r ) } \left | f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right | = \sup _ { \nu _ { m } ( 0 , r ) } \left | \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } \right | .
$$

where

Furthermore, we define Proof. We start by observing that

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \Phi _ { \omega } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) = \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \begin{cases} 1 & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = 0 \\ \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha _ { i } \sim \mathcal { D } _ { i } } \cos ( \alpha _ { i } ) ^ { 2 } \leqslant 1 & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = 1 \\ \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha _ { i } \sim \mathcal { D } _ { i } } \sin ( \alpha _ { i } ) ^ { 2 } \leqslant \sigma ^ { 2 } & \text {if } \omega _ { i } = - 1 \end{cases} .
$$

In order to upper bound the mean square error, we need to estimate how many sine terms are contained in each path ω. Unfortunately, this is highly problem-dependent. For a generic upper bound, we thus choose a maximally-splitting

## 2. Uncorrelated parameters

In this section we consider the case where all the parameters αi are sampled independently from some probability distributions Di supported in [-π, π], and therefore the parameter vector α = (α1, α2, . . . , αm) is sampled from the product distribution D := D1 ×D2 × . . .Dm. For all i, we make following key assumptions on the distribution Di.

1. Symmetric distribution. We assume Di to be symmetric and centered around zero, i.e. for all 0 ⩽ a ⩽ b ⩽ π

$$
\Pr _ { \alpha _ { i } \sim \mathcal { D } _ { i } } \left [ \alpha _ { i } \in [ a , b ] \right ] = \Pr _ { \alpha _ { i } \sim \mathcal { D } _ { i } } \left [ \alpha _ { i } \in [ - b , - a ] \right ]
$$

This assumption ensures that the odd function cos(αi) sin(αi) has mean zero

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha _ { i } \sim \mathcal { D } _ { i } } \cos ( \alpha _ { i } ) \sin ( \alpha _ { i } ) = 0 .
$$

2. Variance bound. The variance of the function sin(αi) is bounded by an appropriately chosen value σ2

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha _ { i } \sim \mathcal { D } _ { i } } \sin ^ { 2 } ( \alpha _ { i } ) \leqslant \sigma ^ { 2 } .
$$

In particular, these two assumptions are satisfied by a truncated Gaussian distribution with mean 0 and variance σ2 supported in [-π, π], and by any symmetric distribution supported entirely in the range [-√3σ, √3σ].

Crucially, the symmetry assumption ensures that the different paths are "orthogonal", in the sense that the associated coefficients are uncorrelated

$$
\omega ^ { \prime } \neq \omega \implies \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) \Phi _ { \omega ^ { \prime } } ( \alpha ) = 0 .
$$

This occurs when for any i one has ωi = 1, ω′i = -1, or vice versa. Given that all paths stem from the same Pauli operator in the Pauli decomposition of the observable O, two different paths must have split off from another, collecting opposing coefficients. This implies that Eα∼D Φω(α)Φω′(α) = Cω\ωi · Eαi∼Di cos(αi) sin(αi) = 0, where Cω\ωi are the integrals over all other independent parameters αj, j ̸= i.

The above observation allows us to drastically simplify the expression of the mean square error:

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left ( \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { K } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } \right ) ^ { 2 } & = \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { K } ^ { m } } \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \Phi _ { \omega } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } ^ { 2 } \\ & < \sum _ { \omega } \mathbb { E } _ { K } \quad \Phi _ { \omega } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha )
$$

$$
\leqslant \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \Phi _ { \omega } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) ,
$$

where we used the fact that d2ω = Tr[Pωρ]2 ⩽ 1 for all ω. Leveraging the variance bound, we can upper bound the mean square error as follows.

Supplemental Theorem 4 (Mean square error for uncorrelated parameters). Let D satisfy the assumptions stated above with σ2 ⩽ κ/m. Then we have

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left [ \left ( f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m \sigma ^ { 2 } } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } .
$$

problem, where any truncation induces the largest loss in probability mass. That is, we assume that the splitting tree yields 2m paths with all combinations of sin and cos prefactors. We can thus write

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left [ \left ( f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] & \leqslant \sum _ { \omega \in S _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \Phi _ { \omega } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha ) \leqslant \sum _ { \substack { \omega \in \{ 1 , - 1 \} ^ { m } \\ \# \sin ( \omega ) \geqslant \kappa } } \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \Phi _ { \omega } ^ { 2 } ( \alpha )
$$

$$
\leqslant \sum _ { i = \kappa } ^ { m } \binom { m } { i } \left ( \sigma ^ { 2 } \right ) ^ { i } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m \sigma ^ { 2 } } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } ,
$$

where #sin(ω) is defined in Eq. (E23) and the last step follows from Lemma 4 since by assumption σ2 ⩽ κ/m.

Uniformly sampled parameters. We now consider the special case of particular interest when all the parameters αi are sampled independently from the uniform distribution over the symmetric interval [-r, r], where r > 0 is a suitable small constant.

Averaging over the small angle range [-r, r] leads to

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha _ { i } \sim \mathcal { D } _ { i } } \sin ( \alpha _ { i } ) ^ { 2 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 r } \int _ { - r } ^ { r } \sin ( \alpha _ { i } ) ^ { 2 } d \alpha _ { i } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left ( 1 - \frac { \sin ( 2 r ) } { 2 r } \right ) \leqslant \frac { r ^ { 2 } } { 3 } \, .
$$

From the previous, we can upper bound the mean square error by setting σ2 = r2 /3 and invoking Supplemental Theorem 4.

Corollary 3 (Mean square error for the uniform distribution). Let D be the uniform distribution over [-r, r]m, where r2 ⩽ 3κ/m. Then we have

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } \left ( f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } .
$$

In particular, for any r ⩽ √1/m, this bound decreases faster than exponentially with κ.

## 3. Worst-case analysis

In this section, we consider the parameter vector α = (α1, α2, . . . , αm) to be arbitrarily chosen - possibly adversarially - in the hypercube [-r, r]m. Thus, we drop the "symmetry assumption" and we don't assume the different αi to be independently sampled.

We consider the maximum error over the hypercube:

$$
\max _ { \alpha \in [ - r , r ] ^ { m } } \left | f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right | .
$$

We provide the following upper bound.

Supplemental Theorem 5 (Worst-case error). Let r ⩽ κ/m. Then we have

$$
\max _ { \alpha \in [ - r , r ] ^ { m } } \left | f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right | & \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m r } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } .
$$

Thus, for any r ⩽ 1/m, the absolute error decreases faster than exponentially in κ.

Proof. We start by upper bounding the error as follows

$$
\left | f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right | = \left | \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } \right | \leqslant \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } | \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) | \ ,
$$

where we used the triangle inequality and the fact |dω| = Tr[Pωρ] ⩽ 1.

Proceeding as in the proof of Supplemental Theorem 4, we obtain

$$
\sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } | \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) | \leqslant \sum _ { \substack { \omega \in \{ 1 , - 1 \} ^ { m } \\ \# \sin ( \omega ) \geqslant \kappa } } | \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) |
$$

$$
\# \sin ( \omega ) \geqslant & \sum _ { \substack { \omega \in \{ 1 , - 1 \} ^ { m } \\ \# \sin ( \omega ) \geqslant \kappa } } r ^ { \# \sin ( \omega ) } = \sum _ { i = \kappa } ^ { m } \binom { m } { i } r ^ { i } \leqslant \left ( \frac { \ e m r } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } ,
$$

where we used the fact that |sin(αi)| ⩽ r, |cos(αi)| ⩽ 1 and we applied Lemma 4 in the last step. Recall that #sin(ω) is defined in Eq. (E23). Notice that the proof is also valid if the αi's are correlated and provides also a bound for the 1-norm of the coefficients of the paths.

## 4. Circuits with correlated parameters

Given the apparent difference in asymptotic scalings between the average case of parameters randomly sampled from a distribution and the worst case, we can ask the question: How hard is it to simulate fundamentally interesting circuits? Here we consider the example of Trotter circuits for simulating quantum dynamics. The scenario is that of perfectly correlated angles, as the same parameter is used across all of the gates. This is the case when all coefficients in a Hamiltonian are equal. Clearly, our earlier worst-case error also bounds this case, but we will now consider the average case of correlated parameters.

Proposition 5 (Improved bound for correlated parameters). Let D the distribution over Vm(α∗ , r) obtained by sampling a parameter α1 uniformly at random over [-r, r] and setting α = (α1, α1, . . . , α1). Then the average error satisfies the following upper bound:

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } \left | f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right | \leqslant \frac { \left ( \frac { e m r } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } } { \kappa + 1 } .
$$

Proof. Here we assume a quantum circuit consisting of Clifford and Pauli gates, where the Pauli gates are all parametrized by the identical angle α1. The average error is

$$
\frac { 1 } { 2 r } \int _ { - r } ^ { r } \left | f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right | d \alpha _ { 1 } = \frac { 1 } { 2 r } \int _ { - r } ^ { r } \left | \sum _ { \omega \in S _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d \omega \right | d \alpha _ { 1 } \\
$$

$$
| \Phi \varsigma _ { \kappa } | & = | \Phi \rangle ( \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } | \, d _ { \alpha _ { 1 } } \\ \leqslant \frac { 1 } { 2 r } \int _ { - r } ^ { r } \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } | \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) d _ { \omega } | \, d _ { \alpha _ { 1 } } \\ \intertext { l e f s } \overline { \sum } _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } | \Psi ( \alpha ) | ^ { 2 } + \dots + \Psi ( \alpha ) | \Psi ( \alpha ) | ^ { 2 } \\
$$

$$
& \leqslant \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \frac { 1 } { 2 r } \int _ { - r } ^ { r } | \alpha _ { 1 } | ^ { \# \sin ( \omega ) } d \alpha _ { 1 } \\ & \quad \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \frac { 1 } { 2 r } \int _ { - r } ^ { r } | \omega _ { - 1 } ( \omega ) | ^ { \# \sin ( \omega ) } d \alpha _ { 1 }
$$

$$
& = \sum _ { \omega \in \mathcal { S } _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \frac { 1 } { r } \int _ { 0 } ^ { r } \alpha _ { 1 } ^ { \# \sin ( \omega ) } d \alpha _ { 1 } \, , \\
$$

where we used the triangle inequality in the first inequality, and | sin(α1)| ⩽ |α1|, | cos(α1)| ⩽ 1, d2ω ⩽ 1 for the second inequality with the same notation for the number of sins per path as in Eq. (E23). In the last equality we use the fact that the integral is symmetric over the absolute value α1, to compute the integral over a positive value range. Again assuming a maximally splitting tree behavior as the worst case yields

$$
\sum _ { \substack { \omega \in \{ 1 , - 1 \} ^ { m } \\ \# \sin ( \omega ) \geq \kappa } } \frac { 1 } { r } \int _ { 0 } ^ { r } \alpha _ { 1 } ^ { \# \sin ( \omega ) } d \alpha _ { 1 } & \leqslant \sum _ { i = \kappa } ^ { m } \binom { m } { i } \frac { 1 } { r } \int _ { 0 } ^ { r } \alpha _ { 1 } ^ { i } d \alpha _ { 1 } \\
$$

$$
= \sum _ { i = \kappa } ^ { m } \binom { m } { i } \frac { r ^ { i } } { i + 1 } \\
$$

$$
\leqslant \sum _ { i = \kappa } ^ { m } \binom { m } { i } \frac { r ^ { i } } { \kappa + 1 } \, ,
$$

where in the end we used that 1 i+1 ⩽ 1 κ+1 for i ⩾ κ. Finally, we can use Lemma 4 to get

$$
\frac { 1 } { 2 r } \int _ { - r } ^ { r } \left | f ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ^ { ( P ) } ( \alpha ) \right | d \alpha _ { 1 } \leqslant \frac { \left ( \frac { e m _ { E } } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } } { \kappa + 1 } \ ,
$$

which corresponds to the upper bound of the worst-case multiplied by a factor 1 κ+1 (see Supplemental Theorem 5).

Looking ahead to Appendix E3, we see that the error scaling is the same for the worst case as in the average case with fully correlated angles. Whether this is a feature of our bounds or indicative of something more fundamental is left as an open question.

## 5. Time complexity of the Pauli Propagation algorithm

Recall that the circuit ansatz defined in Eq. (5) is of the form

$$
U ( \alpha ) = \prod _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } C _ { i } U _ { i } ( \alpha _ { i } ) .
$$

We start by upper bounding the time complexity of the Pauli Propagation algorithm as a function of the system size n, the number of Pauli rotations (and Clifford gates) m and the truncation order κ.

Upper bound on the number of paths: In a quantum circuit with m Pauli rotations as in Eq. (5), the total number of Pauli paths n(m) pth is upper bounded by n(m) pth ⩽ 2m. This is achieved when the backpropagating Pauli operators split into two at every gate. As used throughout this appendix, the distribution of the number of sines in the 2m paths is given via

$$
n _ { p t h } ^ { ( m ) } \leqslant \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { m } \binom { m } { i } = 2 ^ { m } \, ,
$$

where the index i represents the number of sines.

Given that we truncate paths with more than κ sines, the number of paths n(κ) pth that we need to compute is upper-bounded like

$$
n _ { p t h } ^ { ( \kappa ) } \leq \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { \kappa } \binom { m } { i } \leq \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } ,
$$

as stated in Eq. (A7).

Runtime T for updating the Pauli operators: The runtime of the Pauli propagation algorithm is necessarily upperbounded by a number proportional to the number of paths n(κ) pth . We call this proportionality factor T, and it includes the time complexity of updating a propagating Pauli operator when a gate is applied to it. More concretely, let V be either a Clifford Ci or a Pauli rotation Ui(αi). Given a Pauli operator P ∈ Pn we need to upper bound the time complexity for computing V †PV .

Throughout the main text, we have assumed T ∈ O(1) for simplicity, but we here include the explicit dependence on T for preciseness. Interestingly, the case of T ∈ O(1) can be accurate under certain implementation and simulation assumptions. For example, T ∈ O(1) is possible if V acts on O(1) qubits and the Pauli operator P can be updated in-place on a subset of qubits. If in-place manipulation is not done, then the full operator needs to be copied which takes O(n) time. In general, global Clifford gates can be decomposed into O(n2/ log(n)) local Clifford gates [88, 113], each taking O(1) or O(n) time (in-place or not). Furthermore, one could define Ci as an arbitrary long concatenation of local Clifford gates without re-compilation, which can take an arbitrary large amount of time to execute. However, even global Pauli rotations can be applied in time O(n). Thus, in summary, in the best case we have T ∈ O(1) and in the worst T ∈ O(n3 ). Given that we assumed m ∈ O(∈ poly(n)), the extra factor of n3 can be absorbed by big O into our final time complexity. Hence, in the main text we take T ∈ O(1).

Runtime for a truncation order κ: The total number of gates applied to all the paths is

$$
m n _ { p t h } ^ { ( \kappa ) } \leqslant m \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } .
$$

As we assumed that the time for computing the new Pauli terms is T, this leads to a total runtime t of

$$
t \leqslant T m n _ { p t h } ^ { ( \kappa ) } \leqslant T m \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } .
$$

In the following, we provide an upper bound on the runtime required to achieve an error of ϵ, considering both the cases of uncorrelated and worst-case parameters. Moreover, we will now consider more general observable O = ∑P∈Pn aPP by combining previous analysis for single Paulis together with Lemma 8.

Supplemental Theorem 6 (Time complexity of small-angle Pauli propagation (uncorrelated angles), Formal)). Let O = ∑P∈Pn aPP be an observable containing NPaulis different Pauli terms and satisfying ∥a∥1 = O(1). Furthermore, assume that given some P, we can efficiently estimate Tr[ρP]. Let T be the time necessary to apply a gate to a propagating Pauli operator. Then small-angle Pauli propagation can be used to simulate a quantum circuit of the form of Eq. (5) over uniformly sampled zero-centered hypercube of width 2r, i.e. Dm(0, r) with r ∈ O(1/√m). To ensure a mean square error

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } \left [ \left ( f ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant \| a \| _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } \left ( \frac { e m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } = \epsilon ^ { 2 } \, ,
$$

it suffices to run the algorithm for time

$$
t = N _ { P a u l i s } T m \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { P a u l i s } T m ^ { \log ( 1 / \epsilon ^ { 2 } ) } \right ) .
$$

Proof. To approximate the expectation value of the observable O, we will run the Pauli Propagation algorithm for all the Pauli operators in the Pauli expansion of O. Let t̃ be the time required to estimate the expectation value of a single Pauli observable P up to mean square error ˜ ϵ2 = ϵ2/∥a∥21 . Then, invoking Lemma 8, we find that the time required to to estimate the expectation value of O up to mean square error ϵ2 is

$$
\tilde { t } \cdot N _ { \text {Paulis} } .
$$

Given r ∈ O(1/√m), we introduce a constant c ∈ O(1) such that r = c/√m and consider the distribution α randomly sampled from Dm(0, c/√m). Then, as long as the truncation order satisfies

$$
\kappa \geqslant \frac { c ^ { 2 } } { 3 } ,
$$

Corollary 3 implies that, for any given Pauli operator P, the Pauli Propagation algorithm returns an operator P̃α, which is a weighted sum of Pauli operators, satisfying:

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } _ { m } ( 0 , r ) } \left | T r \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { P } _ { \alpha } \rho \right ] \right | ^ { 2 } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e c ^ { 2 } } { 3 \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } .
$$

In order to achieve the desired error, we set

$$
\tilde { \epsilon } ^ { 2 } \coloneqq \left ( \frac { e c ^ { 2 } } { 3 \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa }
$$

$$
\Longrightarrow \kappa \log \kappa \in \Theta ( \log ( 1 / \tilde { \epsilon } ^ { 2 } ) ) .
$$

As observed in Eq. (E56), the number of paths found by the Pauli Propagation algorithm is at most

$$
n _ { p t h } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } = m ^ { \kappa } \cdot \vec { \epsilon } ^ { 2 } \cdot \left ( \frac { 3 } { c ^ { 2 } } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \in \mathcal { O } ( m ^ { \kappa } \vec { \epsilon } ^ { 2 } ) .
$$

Therefore, the runtime for a single Pauli operator in Eq. (E58) is at most

$$
\tilde { t } \leqslant T m \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \in \mathcal { O } ( T m ^ { \kappa + 1 } \tilde { \epsilon } ^ { 2 } ) = T m ^ { \mathcal { O } ( \kappa ) } .
$$

We further observe that, for sufficiently large constant κ,

$$
m ^ { \mathcal { O } ( \kappa ) } = m ^ { \frac { 1 } { \log ( \kappa ) } \mathcal { O } ( \kappa \log ( \kappa ) ) } = m ^ { \frac { \mathcal { O } \left ( \log _ { \ell } ( \| \alpha _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } / \epsilon ^ { 2 } ) \right ) } { \log ( \kappa ) } } = \mathcal { O } \left ( m ^ { \log ( 1 / \epsilon ^ { 2 } ) } \right ) ,
$$

where we recall that ∥a∥21 ∈ O(1). Thus, we conclude that the runtime of the Pauli Propagation algorithm for the observable O can be upper bounded by

$$
t \leqslant N _ { \text {Paulis} } \cdot \tilde { t } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { \text {Paulis} } T \, m ^ { \log ( 1 / \epsilon ^ { 2 } ) } \right ) .
$$

Supplemental Theorem 7 (Time complexity of small-angle Pauli propagation (worst-case)). Let O = ∑P∈Pn aPP be an observable containing NPaulis different Pauli terms and satisfying ∥a∥1 = O(1). Furthermore, assume that given some P, we can efficiently estimate Tr[ρP]. $$

$$
\max _ { \alpha \in \mathcal { V } _ { m } ( 0 , r ) } \left | f ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right | & \leqslant \| a \| _ { 1 } \left ( \frac { e r m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } = \epsilon .
$$

Proof. This proof is similar to that of Supplemental Theorem 6, but here we are considering the worst-case error upper bound. Let us rewrite some part of previous proof for completeness. \tilde { t } \cdot N _ { \text {Paulis} } . \\

$$
\left | \text {Tr} \left [ P U ( \alpha ) \rho U ^ { \dagger } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { P } _ { \alpha } \rho \right ] \right | \leqslant \left ( \frac { e c } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } .

$$
\widetilde { \epsilon } \colon = \left ( \frac { e c } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \\
$$

$$
\Longrightarrow \kappa \log \kappa \in \Theta ( \log ( 1 / \widetilde { \epsilon } ) ) . \\ \\
$$

As observed in Eq. (E56), the number of paths found by the Pauli Propagation algorithm is at most

$$
n _ { p t h } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } = m ^ { \kappa } \cdot \tilde { \epsilon } \cdot \left ( \frac { 1 } { c } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \in \mathcal { O } ( m ^ { \kappa } \widetilde { \epsilon } ) .
$$

And therefore the runtime for a single Pauli operator in Eq. (E58) is at most

$$
\tilde { t } \leqslant T m \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \in \mathcal { O } ( T m ^ { \kappa + 1 } \widehat { \epsilon } ) = T m ^ { \mathcal { O } ( \kappa ) } .
$$

We further observe that, for sufficiently large constant κ,

$$
m ^ { \mathcal { O } ( \kappa ) } = m ^ { \overline { \log ( 1 / \| \alpha \| _ { 1 } / \epsilon ) } } & = m ^ { \frac { \mathcal { O } ( \log ( \| \alpha \| _ { 1 } / \epsilon ) ) } { \log ( \kappa ) } } = \mathcal { O } ( m ^ { \log ( 1 / \epsilon ) } ) \, . \\
$$

Thus, we conclude that the runtime of the Pauli Propagation algorithm for the observable O can be upper bounded by

$$
t \leqslant N _ { \text {Paulis} } \cdot \tilde { t } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { \text {Paulis} } T \, m ^ { \log ( 1 / \epsilon ) } \right ) .
$$

An interesting corollary of our theorem is that in the cases where the worst-case results hold, our simulation method can reduce Trotter error in quantum dynamics simulations with "only" a polynomial slow-down. To reduce Trotter error, one needs to reduce dt and in turn inversely proportionally increases the number of layers L to leave the total simulation time dtL unchanged. That is, if we reduce dt by a factor 1/c we need to increase the number of layers by a factor of c, which in turn increases the number of parameters by a factor c. The maximum dt we can handle with small-angle Pauli Propagation with worst case error guarantees is determined by r. As the error for small-angle Pauli propagation scales in the worse case simulation as mr (this is easiest to see from our formal statement in Theorem 7) we see that reducing Trotter error by reducing dt by a factor 1/c and increasing the total number of layers leaves the final simulation error unchanged. However, the total simulation time increases polynomially in m and so we obtain a polynomial overhead in time complexity of small-angle Pauli Propagation resulting from the total number of parameters increasing by a factor of c. More crudely speaking, if our algorithm can accurately simulate to time t with large time step dt in polynomial time, then it can simulate to time t with small time step dt in polynomial time.

## 6. Sample complexity of the Pauli Propagation algorithm

In the previous section, we demonstrated that Pauli Propagation enables efficient simulation of quantum circuits composed of Clifford gates and Pauli rotations within a small angle range. To upper bound the computational complexity, we crucially assumed that the input state ρ is classically simulable, meaning that for any Pauli operator P, we can efficiently estimate Tr[Pρ].

In the most general case, ρ may be an unknown state that we can access through quantum measurements. In this section, we combine the Pauli Propagation guarantee from Appendix E with the randomized measurement protocol from Appendix B, achieving a polynomial sample complexity for arbitrarily small constant error, i.e., ϵ ∈ Θ(1). We further show that, if the final back-propagated Pauli operators have low Pauli weight, then the number of required Pauli measurements scales logarithmically with the system size.

## a. The truncated Heisenberg-evolved observable

Pauli observables. Given a Pauli operator Q ∈ Pn and a circuit U(α) as input, the Pauli-path simulation algorithm returns the observable

$$
\widetilde { Q } ( \alpha ) & = \sum _ { \omega \notin S _ { \kappa } ^ { m } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) Q _ { \omega } = \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { P } _ { n } } c _ { P } ^ { ( Q ) } ( \alpha ) P , \\ + \ D \ \varphi ^ { \prime } \cdot \ \varphi & = \varphi ^ { \prime } \cdot \ \varphi + 1 \quad \cdot \quad \varphi \colon = \varphi ^ { \prime } \cdot \ \varphi \cdot \ \varphi ^ { \prime } .
$$

where we merged different Pauli paths by introducing the parametrized coefficients

$$
c _ { P } ^ { ( Q ) } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { \substack { \omega \not \in S _ { \Omega } ^ { m } \\ t . \ Q _ { \omega } ^ { S _ { \Omega } ^ { m } } = P } } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) .
$$

Recall that Qω in previous expressions is the Pauli obtained after back-propagating Q along the path ω. For our sample complexity analysis, we need to upper bound the effective 1-norms ∥∥c(Q)∥∥1,avg and ∥∥c(Q)∥∥1,worst introduced in Definition 1. We start by providing a loose, yet polynomial in the number of gates, upper bound. First, we notice that the average-case effective 1-norm is always upper bounded by the worst-case effective 1-norm. Let D be a distribution over the set A. We have

$$
\left \| c ^ { ( Q ) } \right \| _ { 1 , a v g } = \sum _ { P } \sqrt { \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha \sim \mathcal { D } } [ c _ { P } ^ { ( Q ) } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } ] } \leqslant \sum _ { P } \max _ { \alpha \in A } \left | c _ { P } ^ { ( Q ) } ( \alpha ) \right | = \left \| c ^ { ( Q ) } \right \| _ { 1 , w o r s t } .
$$

Furthermore, we can upper bound ∥∥c(Q)∥∥1,worst as done in the proof of Supplemental Theorem 5:

$$
\left \| c ^ { ( Q ) } \right \| _ { 1 , a g } \leqslant \left \| c ^ { ( Q ) } \right \| _ { 1 , w o r s t } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m r } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } = \Theta \left ( ( r m ) ^ { \kappa } \right ) .
$$

Indeed, this is obtained by applying triangle inequality on ∣∣∣c(Q) P (α)∣∣∣ in Eq. (E82) and upper bounding each |Φω(α)| by r#sin(ω) as done in Eq. (E44).

General observables. Let us consider a general observable expressed in the Pauli basis

$$

$$

with ∥a∥1 ∈ O(1). The final back-propagated observables equals

$$

$$

where we introduced the parametrized coefficients

$$
c _ { P } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { Q \in \{ I , X , Y , Z \} ^ { \otimes n } } a _ { Q } c _ { P } ^ { ( Q ) } ( \alpha ) .
$$

Then, we can upper-bound the effective norms as follows:

$$

$$

Proof. From Eq. (E87), we can use triangle inequality to get

$$

$$

## d. Even allocation of shots

Here, we provide a proof of the surrogate guarantee based on Supplemental Theorem 8 using even allocation of shots between the Paulis that needs to be measured instead of the effective 1-norm allocation strategy used in Supplemental Theorem 8. As opposed to the warm-up example presented in Appendix B1, here we fix the number of shots ns for each Pauli that needs to be measured in order to estimate the surrogate of the expectation function. Therefore, the total number of shots is at most Ns = NPaulisn(κ) pthns recalling that NPaulis is the number of Paulis contained by O and n(κ) pth is the number of path as mentioned previously.

Supplemental Theorem 12 (Surrogation via Direct Pauli Measurements, Formal). Consider an observable O = ∑P∈{I,X,Y,Z}⊗n aPP, satisfying ∥a∥1 ∈ O(1) and ∥a∥0 = NPaulis, i.e. O contains NPaulis Pauli terms. Then running a classical Pauli Propagation algorithm in time

$$
t \leqslant N _ { \text {Paulis} } T m \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { \text {Paulis} } T m ^ { \log ( 1 / \epsilon ^ { 2 } ) } \right ) \, ,
$$

$$
N _ { s } = N _ { P a u l i s } \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \in \mathcal { O } \left ( N _ { P a u l i s } m ^ { \log ( 1 / \epsilon ^ { 2 } ) } \right )
$$

randomized Pauli measurements, it is possible to estimate a function ̂f̃κ(α) such that for all r ∈ O(1/√m), we have

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , M } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant \epsilon ^ { 2 } ,
$$

where the expectation is both over the randomness of α ∼Dm(0, r) and the internal randomness of the measurement protocol denoted by M.

Notice that applying Markov's inequality together with Eq. (E133), we can ensure

$$
\left | \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - f ( \alpha ) \right | < \tilde { \epsilon } \, ,
$$

with probability at least 1 - δ (for any δ ∈ Θ(1)) over both α ∼ Dm(0, r) and the measurement output M where ˜ ϵ = ϵ √δ ∈ Θ(1) can be made arbitrarily small by increasing κ ∈ O(1). This leads to the formal version of Theorem 3 stated in the main text.

Proof. The proof here is the same as in Supplemental Theorem 8 except the surrogation step (i.e., empirical error estimation). In the following, we show that for uniform allocation of shots Eq. (E102) can be replaced by

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \alpha , \mathcal { M } } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant \frac { N _ { P a u l i s } \| a \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } } { N _ { s } } \left ( \frac { e m r } { \sqrt { 3 } \kappa } \right ) ^ { 2 \kappa } \, .
$$

The propagated observable can be written as

$$
\widetilde { O } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { \substack { P \in \mathcal { P } _ { n } ^ { n } \\ a _ { P } \neq 0 } } \sum _ { \# \sin ( \omega ) > \kappa } a _ { P } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) P _ { \omega } \, ,
$$

where Pω is the Pauli obtained after back-propagating P along the path ω. A worst-case scenario where each Pω are different Paulis is considered here (that is, we don't group coefficients that corresponds to the same Pauli). So, for each Pauli P and each path ω considered (i.e. each Pω), we will use ns shots to estimate Tr[ρPω]. Let x̂(i) P,ω denotes the i-th measurement outcome obtained by measuring Tr[ρPω] with the quantum computer. Then, our empirical estimate of Tr[ρPω] for each Pω is simply the average over the ns corresponding measurement outcomes

$$
\frac { 1 } { n _ { s } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n _ { s } } \hat { x } _ { P , \omega } ^ { ( i ) } \, .
$$

Therefore, the empirical estimate for the surrogate of the expectation function is given by

$$
\widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { \substack { P \in \mathcal { P } _ { n } \\ a _ { P } \neq 0 } } \sum _ { \omega } \sum _ { \substack { i = 1 \\ i \neq n } } ^ { n _ { s } } \frac { a _ { P } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) } { n _ { s } } \hat { x } _ { P , \omega } ^ { ( i ) } \, , \\
$$

which is an unbiased estimator for f̃κ(α). Moreover, notice that the x̂(i) P,ω are uncorrelated (independent measurement). From the previous, the mean square error (over both the parameters α and the measurement process denoted by M) is given by

$$
\mathbb { E } _ { \mathcal { M } , \alpha } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] = \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } \left [ \text {Var} _ { \mathcal { M } } \left [ \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right ] \right ]
$$

$$
\int \lim i t s _ { \substack { E \mathbb { Z } _ { \alpha } \left [ \sum _ { \substack { P \in \mathcal { P } _ { n } ^ { n } \neq \sin ( \omega ) > \kappa } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n _ { s } } \frac { a _ { P } ^ { 2 } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } } { n _ { s } ^ { 2 } } V a r _ { \mathcal { M } } \left [ \hat { x } _ { P , \omega } ^ { ( i ) } \right ] \right ] \\ a _ { P } \neq \sin ( \omega ) > \kappa } } \\
$$

$$
& \leqslant \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } \left [ \sum _ { \substack { P \in \mathbb { P } _ { n } ^ { n } \notin \sin ( \omega ) > \kappa } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n _ { s } } \frac { a _ { P } ^ { 2 } \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } } { n _ { s } ^ { 2 } } \right ] \\ & 1 \sum _ { \substack { P \in \mathbb { P } _ { n } ^ { n } \notin \sin ( \omega ) > \kappa } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n _ { s } } \mathbb { F } _ { \ } [ \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } ]
$$

$$
= \frac { 1 } { n _ { s } } \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { P } _ { n } } a _ { P } ^ { 2 } \sum _ { \# \sin ( \omega ) > \kappa } \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } [ \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } ] \, ,
$$

where the inequality is obtained by noticing that VarM [ x̂(i) P,ω ] = 1 -Tr[ρPω]2 ⩽ 1. Moreover, from Supplemental Theorem 4 or more precisely from Corollary 3, we have

$$
\sum _ { \# \sin ( \omega ) > \kappa } \mathbb { E } _ { \alpha } \left [ \Phi _ { \omega } ( \alpha ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m r ^ { 2 } } { 3 \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \, .
$$

The total number of shots is given by Ns = nsNPaulis n(κ) pth where we recall that NPaulis is the number of initial Paulis (i.e., the number of Pauli P such that aP ̸= 0) and n(κ) pth is the number of Pauli path in the surrogate (the number of path ω such that #sin(ω) > κ). From Eq. (E56), we have

$$
n _ { p t h } ^ { ( \kappa ) } \leqslant \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \, .
$$

Therefore, combining everything together leads to the following upper bound for the mean square empirical error

$$
\mathbb { E } \left [ \left ( \widehat { \widetilde { f } } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) - \widetilde { f } _ { \kappa } ( \alpha ) \right ) ^ { 2 } \right ] \leqslant \frac { N _ { P a u l i s } \left \| a \right \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } } { N _ { s } } \left ( \frac { e m r } { \sqrt { 3 } \kappa } \right ) ^ { 2 \kappa } \, .
$$

Finally, substituting this in the proof of Supplemental Theorem 8, we get that the total number of shots required for even allocation is given by

$$
N _ { s } = \frac { \| a \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } N _ { P a u l i s } } { \| a \| _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } } \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \, .
$$

$$
N _ { s } = N _ { P a u i s } \left ( \frac { e m } { \kappa } \right ) ^ { \kappa } \, ,
$$

is sufficient to get the same error guarantee as in Supplemental Theorem 8.

Notice here that with even allocation of shots we get a 2-norm instead of a 1-norm of the coefficients of the initial observable. However, we also get the additional factor NPaulis. Let us consider the warm-up example presented in Appendix B1 where the initial observable is given by

$$
O = Z ^ { \otimes n } + \frac { \tau } { 4 ^ { n } - 1 } \sum _ { P \in \mathcal { P } _ { n } \ \{ Z ^ { \otimes n } \} } P \, ,
$$

where τ ∈ O(1). Therefore, we have ∥a∥21 = (1 + τ)2 ∈ O(1) and ∥a∥21 = 1 + τ 2 4n-1 ∈ O(1), but NPaulis = 4n . Therefore, the number of shots required with even allocation (provided by the bound) is exponentially larger than the effective 1-norm allocation. In fact, this will highly depends on the number of Paulis in the initial observable. As we are mainly considering O(poly(n)) Pauli in the observable, both bounds remains polynomial with n and m.

## 7. Additional Numerical Results

To better elucidate the system-dependence of the PP surrogation in Fig. 9, we here showcase the same numerical experiment with a Heisenberg model Hamiltonian variational ansatz (HVA) instead of a transverse-field Ising (TFI) HVA. This ansatz not only contains more Pauli rotations, but also more and larger groups of commuting operators. On the one hand, this leads to more Pauli strings being created during the classical simulation, in turn putting more strain on both the classical and quantum components. Generally speaking, the errors at the same resources are larger than in Fig. 4. However, we also see the Pauli shadow estimation strategy becoming superior to the 1-norm strategy, likely due to the increased structure and number of commuting terms. A notable difference between shadows and our 1-norm allocation is that the latter does not group commuting Pauli strings together for simultaneous estimation, which the shadow strategy implicitly does. This structure can be leveraged in sophisticated estimation strategies to design the best quantum-enhanced classical surrogate computation.

Therefore, by taking at least
